\(\bar x=\mu\) and \(\sqrt c\) in place of \(c\), it describes the set where this equals \(c\).\qedhere
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{r11-exm-bivariate-density}
Let \(n=2\), \(\sigma_1,\sigma_2>0\), \(-1<\rho<1\), and
\(\Sigma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\sigma_1^2&\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2\\\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2&\sigma_2^2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\).
Then \(\sigma_1^2>0\) and \(\det\Sigma=\sigma_1^2\sigma_2^2(1-\rho^2)>0\), so \(\Sigma\) is positive definite
(Theorem~\ref{r8-thm-sylvester}), and \(x\sim N_2(\mu,\Sigma)\) has \(\var(x_j)=\sigma_j^2\) and
\(\corr(x_1,x_2)=\rho\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-construct}). By Proposition~\ref{r2-prop-inverse-2x2},
\[
\Sigma^{-1}=\frac1{\sigma_1^2\sigma_2^2(1-\rho^2)}
\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_2^2&-\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2\\-\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2&\sigma_1^2\end{pmatrix}
\]
With \(v_j=(u_j-\mu_j)/\sigma_j\), Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-density} gives the \textbf{bivariate normal
density}
\[
f(u_1,u_2)=\frac1{2\pi\sigma_1\sigma_2\sqrt{1-\rho^2}}
\exp\Bigl(-\frac{v_1^2-2\rho v_1v_2+v_2^2}{2(1-\rho^2)}\Bigr)
\]
For instance, for \(\mu=0\), \(\sigma_1=\sigma_2=1\) and \(\rho=\frac12\),
\(f(u)=\frac1{\pi\sqrt3}\exp\bigl(-\frac23(u_1^2-u_1u_2+u_2^2)\bigr)\). This \(\Sigma\) has the eigenvalues
\(\frac32\), \(\frac12\) with eigenvectors \((1,1)'\), \((1,-1)'\), so the contours of \(f\) are ellipses with
the long axis on the line \(u_1=u_2\) and semi-axes in the ratio \(\sqrt3:1\).
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}\label{r11-thm-mvn-independence}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Let \(x=(x_1',x_2')'\sim N_n(\mu,\Sigma)\), with \(\mu=(\mu_1',\mu_2')'\) and
\(\Sigma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\Sigma_{11}&\Sigma_{12}\\\Sigma_{21}&\Sigma_{22}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\)
partitioned conformally. Then \(x_1\indep x_2\) if and only if \(\Sigma_{12}=0\).
\item Let \(x\sim N_n(\mu,\Sigma)\), \(A\in\R^{p\times n}\) and \(B\in\R^{q\times n}\). Then \(Ax\indep Bx\) if and
only if \(A\Sigma B'=0\).
\item The components of \(x\sim N_n(\mu,D)\) with \(D\) diagonal are independent. More generally, if
\(x=(x_1',\dots,x_m')'\sim N_n(\mu,\Sigma)\) and the blocks \(\Sigma_{ij}=\cov(x_i,x_j)\) are \(0\) for
\(i\ne j\), then \(x_1,\dots,x_m\) are independent.
\item Conversely, if \(x_1,\dots,x_m\) are independent and \(x_i\sim N_{n_i}(\mu_i,\Sigma_i)\), then
\((x_1',\dots,x_m')'\sim N_n\bigl(\mu,\diag(\Sigma_1,\dots,\Sigma_m)\bigr)\), with
\(\mu=(\mu_1',\dots,\mu_m')'\) and \(n=n_1+\dots+n_m\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(c) Partition \(\mu=(\mu_1',\dots,\mu_m')'\) and \(t=(t_1',\dots,t_m')'\) in the same way as \(x\). By the
block product (Theorem~\ref{r5b-thm-block}(c)), \(t'\mu=\sum_it_i'\mu_i\) and
\(t'\Sigma t=\sum_i\sum_jt_i'\Sigma_{ij}t_j=\sum_it_i'\Sigma_{ii}t_i\). So
\[
M_x(t)=\prod_{i=1}^m\exp\bigl(t_i'\mu_i+\tfrac12t_i'\Sigma_{ii}t_i\bigr)=\prod_{i=1}^mM_{x_i}(t_i),
\]
since \(x_i\sim N(\mu_i,\Sigma_{ii})\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(b)). All these MGFs are finite, and
(P3) gives the independence. For \(N_n(\mu,D)\) take blocks of size \(1\).

(a) If \(\Sigma_{12}=0\), apply (c) with \(m=2\). If \(x_1\indep x_2\), then \(\Sigma_{12}=\cov(x_1,x_2)=0\)
(Theorems~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-construct} and~\ref{r11-thm-moments}(e)).

(b) By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a), \(y=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\\B\end{smallmatrix}\right)x\) is normal
with covariance matrix
\(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\\B\end{smallmatrix}\right)\Sigma\,[A'\ B']
=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A\Sigma A'&A\Sigma B'\\B\Sigma A'&B\Sigma B'\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), and its two
blocks are \(Ax\) and \(Bx\). Apply (a) to \(y\).

(d) By (P1), \(\E e^{t'x}=\E\prod_ie^{t_i'x_i}=\prod_i\E e^{t_i'x_i}
=\exp\bigl(\sum_i(t_i'\mu_i+\frac12t_i'\Sigma_it_i)\bigr)\), which is
\(\exp\bigl(t'\mu+\frac12t'\diag(\Sigma_1,\dots,\Sigma_m)t\bigr)\). The matrix
\(\diag(\Sigma_1,\dots,\Sigma_m)\) is symmetric, and positive semidefinite since
\(t'\diag(\Sigma_1,\dots,\Sigma_m)t=\sum_it_i'\Sigma_it_i\geqslant0\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{r11-cor-orthogonal-invariance}
Let \(z\sim N_n(0,\sigma^2I_n)\), and let \(Q\in\R^{n\times n}\) be orthogonal. Then \(Qz\sim N_n(0,\sigma^2I_n)\).
More generally, if \(Q_1\in\R^{n\times r}\) has orthonormal columns, then \(Q_1'z\sim N_r(0,\sigma^2I_r)\), and its
\(r\) entries are independent with distribution \(N(0,\sigma^2)\).
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a), \(Qz\sim N_n(0,\sigma^2QQ')\) and \(Q_1'z\sim N_r(0,\sigma^2Q_1'Q_1)\),
where \(QQ'=I_n\) (Proposition~\ref{r6-prop-orthonormal}(b)) and \(Q_1'Q_1=I_r\). The entries of \(Q_1'z\) are
independent by Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(c), and each is \(N(0,\sigma^2)\) by
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(b).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{r11-rem-uncorrelated}
Joint normality cannot be dropped from Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(a).
\begin{itemize}
\item \textit{Uncorrelated, not independent.} Let \(u\) take the values \(-1,0,1\) with probability \(\frac13\)
each, and \(v=u^2\). Then \(\E u=0\) and \(\E u^3=0\) (P1), so \(\cov(u,v)=\E(uv)-\E u\,\E v=\E u^3=0\). But
\(\Prob(u=0,\ v=1)=0\ne\frac13\cdot\frac23=\Prob(u=0)\Prob(v=1)\), so \(u\) and \(v\) are not independent.
\item \textit{Normal and uncorrelated, not jointly normal.} Let \(x\sim N(0,1)\), let \(s\) be independent of
\(x\) with \(\Prob(s=1)=\Prob(s=-1)=\frac12\), and put \(y=sx\). Let \(\iota_\pm\) be \(1\) if \(s=\pm1\) and
\(0\) otherwise, so that \(\E\iota_\pm=\frac12\) and \(e^{ty}=\iota_+e^{tx}+\iota_-e^{-tx}\). By (P1) and (P5),
\(\E e^{ty}=\frac12e^{t^2/2}+\frac12e^{t^2/2}=e^{t^2/2}\), so \(y\sim N(0,1)\); and
\(\cov(x,y)=\E(sx^2)=\E s\,\E(x^2)=0\). If \(x\) and \(y\) were jointly normal, then \(x+y\) would be normal
with mean \(0\) and variance \(2\) (Theorems~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(b), \ref{r11-thm-moments}(d)), with
MGF \(e^{t^2}\). But \(x+y=(1+s)x\), and in the same way \(\E e^{t(x+y)}=\frac12e^{2t^2}+\frac12\), which at
\(t=1\) is \(\frac12e^2+\frac12\ne e\). So \(x\) and \(y\) are each normal and uncorrelated, but not jointly
normal, and by Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(d) they are not independent.
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}\label{r11-thm-mvn-conditional}
Let \(x=(x_1',x_2')'\sim N_n(\mu,\Sigma)\), partitioned as in Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(a), with
\(\Sigma_{22}\) positive definite, and put
\[
B=\Sigma_{12}\Sigma_{22}^{-1},\qquad\Sigma_{11\cdot2}=\Sigma_{11}-\Sigma_{12}\Sigma_{22}^{-1}\Sigma_{21}
\]
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(w=x_1-Bx_2\) is independent of \(x_2\), and \(w\sim N(\mu_1-B\mu_2,\Sigma_{11\cdot2})\).
\item The conditional distribution of \(x_1\) given \(x_2=a\) is \(N(\mu_1+B(a-\mu_2),\Sigma_{11\cdot2})\). So
the conditional mean \(\E(x_1\mid x_2=a)=\mu_1+B(a-\mu_2)\) is linear in \(a\), and the conditional covariance
matrix \(\var(x_1\mid x_2=a)=\Sigma_{11\cdot2}\) does not depend on \(a\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(a) \(w=[I\ \ {-B}]\,x\) and \(x_2=[0\ \ I]\,x\). By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a), \(w\) is normal with
mean \(\mu_1-B\mu_2\). By the block product, with \(\Sigma_{21}=\Sigma_{12}'\) and \(\Sigma_{22}^{-1}\) symmetric,
\[
\begin{aligned}
[I\ \ {-B}]\,\Sigma\,[0\ \ I]'&=\Sigma_{12}-B\Sigma_{22}=0,\\
[I\ \ {-B}]\,\Sigma\,[I\ \ {-B}]'&=\Sigma_{11}-B\Sigma_{21}-\Sigma_{12}B'+B\Sigma_{22}B'
=\Sigma_{11}-\Sigma_{12}\Sigma_{22}^{-1}\Sigma_{21}
\end{aligned}
\]
The first identity and Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(b) give \(w\indep x_2\); the second is the
covariance matrix of \(w\).

(b) \(x_1=w+Bx_2\) with \(w\indep x_2\). By (P6), the conditional distribution of \(x_1\) given \(x_2=a\) is the
distribution of \(w+Ba\), which is \(N(\mu_1-B\mu_2+Ba,\Sigma_{11\cdot2})\) by
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a).
\end{proof}
For a single variable \(x_1=y\) and \(x_2=x\in\R^k\), \(B=\sigma_{yx}\Sigma_{xx}^{-1}=b^{*\prime}\) and
\(\Sigma_{11\cdot2}=\sigma_{yy}-\sigma_{yx}\Sigma_{xx}^{-1}\sigma_{xy}\) in the notation of
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-blp}: under joint normality the conditional mean \(\E(y\mid x=a)=a^*+b^{*\prime}a\) is the
best linear predictor, and the conditional variance is its mean squared error.

\begin{example}\label{r11-exm-conditional}
Let \((y,x_1,x_2,x_3)'\) be normal with the mean and the covariance matrix of Example~\ref{r11-exm-blp}.
\begin{itemize}
\item Given \(x=(x_1,x_2,x_3)'=a\): \(B=b^{*\prime}=(1,1,1)\) and \(\Sigma_{11\cdot2}=12-10=2\), so
\(y\mid x=a\sim N\bigl(8+(a_1-1)+(a_2-2)+(a_3-3),\ 2\bigr)=N(2+a_1+a_2+a_3,\ 2)\). For instance, given
\(x=(2,3,5)'\), \(y\sim N(12,2)\). Equivalently, by Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-conditional}(a),
\(y=2+x_1+x_2+x_3+u\) with \(u=y-2-x_1-x_2-x_3\sim N(0,2)\) independent of \(x\).
\item Given \(y=c\): \(B=\sigma_{xy}/\sigma_{yy}=\frac1{12}(3,4,3)'\) and
\[
\Sigma_{xx}-\frac1{12}\sigma_{xy}\sigma_{yx}
=\begin{pmatrix}2&1&0\\1&2&1\\0&1&2\end{pmatrix}-\frac1{12}\begin{pmatrix}9&12&9\\12&16&12\\9&12&9\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}5/4&0&-3/4\\0&2/3&0\\-3/4&0&5/4\end{pmatrix},
\]
so \(x\mid y=c\sim N_3\bigl((1,2,3)'+\frac{c-8}{12}(3,4,3)',\ \Sigma_{xx}-\frac1{12}\sigma_{xy}\sigma_{yx}\bigr)\).
\item The variables \(x_1\) and \(x_3\) are uncorrelated, hence independent
(Theorems~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(b) and~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(a)); for each value of \(y\) their
conditional covariance is \(-\frac34\). The variables \(x_1\) and \(x_2\) have covariance \(1\), but for each value
of \(y\) their conditional covariance is \(0\), so under the conditional distribution given \(y=c\) they are
independent (Theorems~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(b) and~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(c), applied to the subvector
\((x_1,x_2)'\) of a random vector with that distribution).
\end{itemize}
\end{example}

\begin{example}\label{r11-exm-bivariate}
Let \((x,y)'\) be bivariate normal with means \(\mu_x,\mu_y\), standard deviations \(\sigma_x,\sigma_y>0\) and
correlation \(\rho\). Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-conditional} with \(x_1=y\), \(x_2=x\) and
\(B=\rho\sigma_x\sigma_y/\sigma_x^2\) gives
\[
y\mid x=a\ \sim\ N\Bigl(\mu_y+\rho\frac{\sigma_y}{\sigma_x}(a-\mu_x),\ \sigma_y^2(1-\rho^2)\Bigr)
\]
The line \(a\mapsto\mu_y+\rho(\sigma_y/\sigma_x)(a-\mu_x)\) is the \textbf{line of conditional means} of \(y\)
given \(x\), and knowing \(x\) multiplies the variance of \(y\) by \(1-\rho^2\). In standard units,
\[
\frac{\E(y\mid x=a)-\mu_y}{\sigma_y}=\rho\,\frac{a-\mu_x}{\sigma_x}
\]
If \(\lvert\rho\rvert<1\) and \(a\ne\mu_x\), the conditional mean of \(y\) lies fewer standard deviations from
\(\mu_y\) than \(a\) lies from \(\mu_x\). Galton observed this in the heights of parents and their adult children
and called it \textbf{regression to the mean}. It holds in both directions: exchanging the roles of \(x\) and
\(y\) gives \((\E(x\mid y=c)-\mu_x)/\sigma_x=\rho(c-\mu_y)/\sigma_y\).
\end{example}

\subsection*{Quadratic forms in normal vectors}

\begin{theorem}\label{r11-thm-chisq}
Let \(z\sim N_n(0,I_n)\), and let \(A\in\R^{n\times n}\) be symmetric and idempotent of rank \(r\geqslant1\).
Then \(z'Az\sim\chi^2_r\).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\begin{itemize}
\item By Proposition~\ref{r8-prop-idempotent}(a), \(A=Q_1Q_1'\) for some \(Q_1\in\R^{n\times r}\) with
\(Q_1'Q_1=I_r\).
\item By Corollary~\ref{r11-cor-orthogonal-invariance}, \(w=Q_1'z\) has independent entries
\(w_1,\dots,w_r\) with distribution \(N(0,1)\). So \(z'Az=(Q_1'z)'(Q_1'z)=w_1^2+\dots+w_r^2\sim\chi^2_r\) by
(P5).\qedhere
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{r11-cor-chisq}
\begin{enumerate}
\item If \(x\sim N_n(0,\sigma^2I_n)\) with \(\sigma>0\), and \(P\in\R^{n\times n}\) is symmetric and idempotent of
rank \(r\geqslant1\), then \(x'Px/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2_r\).
\item If \(x\sim N_n(\mu,\Sigma)\) with \(\Sigma\) positive definite, then
\((x-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(x-\mu)\sim\chi^2_n\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
(a) \(z=x/\sigma\sim N_n(0,I_n)\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a)), and \(x'Px/\sigma^2=z'Pz\). Apply
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-chisq}.

(b) \(\Sigma^{-1/2}\) is symmetric, \(\Sigma^{-1/2}\Sigma\Sigma^{-1/2}=I_n\) and
\(\Sigma^{-1/2}\Sigma^{-1/2}=\Sigma^{-1}\)
(Theorem~\ref{r8-thm-sqrt}(c)). So
\(z=\Sigma^{-1/2}(x-\mu)\sim N_n(0,I_n)\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a)), and
\((x-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(x-\mu)=z'I_nz\sim\chi^2_n\) by Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-chisq}.
\end{proof}
In terms of the Mahalanobis distance \(d_\Sigma\) (Definition~\ref{r8-def-mahalanobis}), (b) says that
\(d_\Sigma(x,\mu)^2\sim\chi^2_n\). Hence the ellipsoid \(\{u:(u-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(u-\mu)\leqslant\chi^2_{n,1-\alpha}\}\)
of Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-density} contains \(x\) with probability \(1-\alpha\). For \(n=2\), (P5) gives
\(\Prob\bigl(d_\Sigma(x,\mu)^2\leqslant c\bigr)=1-e^{-c/2}\); this equals \(1-\alpha\) for \(c=2\log(1/\alpha)\), so
\(\chi^2_{2,1-\alpha}=2\log(1/\alpha)\), for instance \(\chi^2_{2,1-\alpha}=2\log20\) for \(\alpha=\frac1{20}\).

\begin{theorem}\label{r11-thm-quadratic-independence}
Let \(z\sim N_n(0,\sigma^2I_n)\) with \(\sigma>0\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item For \(A\in\R^{p\times n}\) and \(B\in\R^{q\times n}\), \(Az\indep Bz\) if and only if \(AB'=0\).
\item If \(P_1,P_2\in\R^{n\times n}\) are symmetric and idempotent and \(P_1P_2=0\), then
\(z'P_1z\indep z'P_2z\).
\item If \(P\in\R^{n\times n}\) is symmetric and idempotent and \(B\in\R^{q\times n}\) satisfies \(BP=0\), then
\(Bz\indep z'Pz\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(a) By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(b), \(Az\indep Bz\) if and only if
\(A(\sigma^2I_n)B'=\sigma^2AB'=0\).

(b) \(z'P_iz=z'P_i'P_iz=\lVert P_iz\rVert^2\) is a function of \(P_iz\), and \(P_1z\indep P_2z\) by (a), as
\(P_1P_2'=P_1P_2=0\). Apply (P1).

(c) \(z'Pz=\lVert Pz\rVert^2\), and \(Bz\indep Pz\) by (a), as \(BP'=BP=0\). Apply (P1).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Cochran]\label{r11-thm-cochran}
Let \(A_1,\dots,A_k\in\R^{n\times n}\) be symmetric with \(A_1+\dots+A_k=I_n\), let \(r_i=\rank A_i\geqslant1\),
and suppose that \(r_1+\dots+r_k=n\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item Every \(A_i\) is idempotent, and \(A_iA_j=0\) for \(i\ne j\).
\item If \(z\sim N_n(0,\sigma^2I_n)\) with \(\sigma>0\), then \(z'A_1z/\sigma^2,\dots,z'A_kz/\sigma^2\) are
independent, and \(z'A_iz/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2_{r_i}\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(a)
\begin{itemize}
\item For each \(i\), Corollary~\ref{r8-cor-spectral-decomposition}(a),(b) and Theorem~\ref{r1-thm-views}(d)
give \(A_i=U_i\Lambda_iU_i'\), where \(\Lambda_i\) is the diagonal matrix of the \(r_i\) nonzero eigenvalues of
\(A_i\) (listed with multiplicity) and the columns of \(U_i\in\R^{n\times r_i}\) are corresponding orthonormal
eigenvectors of \(A_i\); so \(U_i'U_i=I_{r_i}\), and \(\Lambda_i\) is invertible.
\item Let \(U=[U_1\ \cdots\ U_k]\), an \(n\times n\) matrix since \(\sum_ir_i=n\), and
\(\Lambda=\diag(\Lambda_1,\dots,\Lambda_k)\). By the block product, \(I_n=\sum_iA_i=U\Lambda U'\). So
\(n=\rank I_n\leqslant\rank U\) (Theorem~\ref{thm:r4-rank-product}(a)), and \(U\) is invertible
(Theorem~\ref{thm:r4-rank-rules}(d)).
\item The matrix \(G=U'U\) is invertible (Theorem~\ref{r2-thm-inverse-props}(b),(c)). Multiplying
\(I_n=U\Lambda U'\) on the left by \(U'\) and on the right by \(U\) gives \(G=G\Lambda G\); multiplying by
\(G^{-1}\) on the left gives \(I_n=\Lambda G\), so \(G=\Lambda^{-1}=\diag(\Lambda_1^{-1},\dots,\Lambda_k^{-1})\) (Theorem~\ref{r2-thm-invertible}).
\item The \((i,j)\) block of \(G\) is \(U_i'U_j\), and \(U_i'U_i=I_{r_i}\). Comparing blocks, \(\Lambda_i^{-1}=I_{r_i}\)
and \(U_i'U_j=0\) for \(i\ne j\). Hence \(A_i=U_iU_i'\), \(A_i^2=U_i(U_i'U_i)U_i'=A_i\), and
\(A_iA_j=U_i(U_i'U_j)U_j'=0\) for \(i\ne j\).
\end{itemize}
(b) By (a), \(U'U=G=I_n\): \(U\) is orthogonal, and \(w=U'z\sim N_n(0,\sigma^2I_n)\)
(Corollary~\ref{r11-cor-orthogonal-invariance}). Its blocks \(w_i=U_i'z\in\R^{r_i}\) have zero
cross-covariances, so they are independent (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(c)), and
\(w_i\sim N_{r_i}(0,\sigma^2I_{r_i})\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(b)). Now
\(z'A_iz=z'U_iU_i'z=\lVert w_i\rVert^2\), and \(\lVert w_i\rVert^2/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2_{r_i}\)
(Corollary~\ref{r11-cor-chisq}(a) with \(P=I_{r_i}\)). These are functions of the independent \(w_i\), hence
independent (P1).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{r11-thm-one-sample}
Let \(x_1,\dots,x_n\) be independent with distribution \(N(\mu,\sigma^2)\), where \(n\geqslant2\) and
\(\sigma>0\), and let \(\bar x=\frac1n\sum_ix_i\) and \(s^2=\frac1{n-1}\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)^2\). Then
\[
\bar x\sim N\Bigl(\mu,\frac{\sigma^2}n\Bigr),\qquad\frac{(n-1)s^2}{\sigma^2}\sim\chi^2_{n-1},\qquad
\bar x\indep s^2,\qquad\frac{\sqrt n(\bar x-\mu)}s\sim t_{n-1}
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\begin{itemize}
\item By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(d), \(x=(x_1,\dots,x_n)'\sim N_n(\mu\one,\sigma^2I_n)\). So
\(\varepsilon=x-\mu\one\sim N_n(0,\sigma^2I_n)\) and \(\bar x=\frac1n\one'x\sim N(\mu,\sigma^2/n)\)
(Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a),(b)).
\item \((n-1)s^2=x'C_nx\) (Example~\ref{r1-exm-centering}), and \(C_n\one=0\), so
\((n-1)s^2=\varepsilon'C_n\varepsilon\). As \(C_n\) is symmetric and idempotent of rank \(n-1\geqslant1\)
(Proposition~\ref{r1-prop-centering}, Corollary~\ref{r6-cor-centering}(b)), Corollary~\ref{r11-cor-chisq}(a)
gives \((n-1)s^2/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2_{n-1}\).
\item \(\bar x-\mu=\frac1n\one'\varepsilon\), and \(\frac1n\one'C_n=\frac1n(C_n\one)'=0\). By
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-quadratic-independence}(c), \(\bar x-\mu\indep\varepsilon'C_n\varepsilon\), hence
\(\bar x\indep s^2\) (P1).
\item So \(u=\sqrt n(\bar x-\mu)/\sigma\sim N(0,1)\) and \(v=(n-1)s^2/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2_{n-1}\) are independent
(P1), and \(u/\sqrt{v/(n-1)}=\sqrt n(\bar x-\mu)/s\sim t_{n-1}\) by (P5). (Here \(s>0\) with probability
\(1\), since \(\Prob(v=0)=0\) by (P5).)\qedhere
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{r11-exm-t-interval}
A \textbf{confidence interval} with level \(1-\alpha\) (\(0<\alpha<1\)) for a parameter \(\theta\) is an
interval \([L,U]\) whose endpoints are computed from the observations and satisfy
\(\Prob(L\leqslant\theta\leqslant U)=1-\alpha\) for all values of the parameters. In
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-one-sample}, let \(u=\sqrt n(\bar x-\mu)/\sigma\sim N(0,1)\) and
\(v=(n-1)s^2/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2_{n-1}\), independent by Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-one-sample} and (P1), and let
\(T=\sqrt n(\bar x-\mu)/s=u/\sqrt{v/(n-1)}\) and \(c=t_{n-1,1-\alpha/2}\).
\begin{itemize}
\item \(-T\) also has the distribution \(t_{n-1}\): \(-u\sim N(0,1)\), since
\(\E e^{\lambda(-u)}=e^{(-\lambda)^2/2}=e^{\lambda^2/2}\) for all \(\lambda\in\R\), and \(-u\indep v\) (P1), so \(-T=(-u)/\sqrt{v/(n-1)}\sim t_{n-1}\) by
(P5).
\item Here \(c>0\): \(\Prob(T\leqslant0)=\Prob(-T\leqslant0)=\Prob(T\geqslant0)\), and these two numbers add up to
\(1+\Prob(T=0)=1\) (P5), so \(\Prob(T\leqslant0)=\frac12<1-\frac\alpha2=\Prob(T\leqslant c)\), whereas \(c\leqslant0\)
would give \(\{T\leqslant c\}\subseteq\{T\leqslant0\}\). Hence \(\{T>c\}\) and \(\{T<-c\}\) are disjoint. By (P5),
\(\Prob(T>c)=1-\Prob(T\leqslant c)=\frac\alpha2\), and \(\Prob(T<-c)=\Prob(-T>c)=\frac\alpha2\). So
\(\Prob(-c\leqslant T\leqslant c)=1-\alpha\).
\item \(-c\leqslant T\leqslant c\) if and only if \(\bar x-cs/\sqrt n\leqslant\mu\leqslant\bar x+cs/\sqrt n\). So
\(\bigl[\bar x-t_{n-1,1-\alpha/2}\,s/\sqrt n,\ \bar x+t_{n-1,1-\alpha/2}\,s/\sqrt n\bigr]\) is a confidence
interval for \(\mu\) with level \(1-\alpha\).
\item Let \(c_1=\chi^2_{n-1,\alpha/2}\) and \(c_2=\chi^2_{n-1,1-\alpha/2}\). By (P5),
\(\Prob(c_1\leqslant v\leqslant c_2)=\Prob(v\leqslant c_2)-\Prob(v<c_1)=(1-\frac\alpha2)-\frac\alpha2=1-\alpha\), since
\(\Prob(v=c_1)=0\). As \(c_1\leqslant v\leqslant c_2\) if and only if \((n-1)s^2/c_2\leqslant\sigma^2\leqslant(n-1)s^2/c_1\),
\(\bigl[(n-1)s^2/\chi^2_{n-1,1-\alpha/2},\ (n-1)s^2/\chi^2_{n-1,\alpha/2}\bigr]\) is a confidence interval for
\(\sigma^2\) with level \(1-\alpha\).
\end{itemize}
For the nine measurements \(5,7,9,9,10,11,11,13,15\), modeled as independent \(N(\mu,\sigma^2)\):
\(\bar x=10\), \(\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)^2=25+9+1+1+0+1+1+9+25=72\), \(s^2=72/8=9\) and \(s=3\). So
\(s/\sqrt n=1\), and the interval with level \(1-\alpha\) is
\(\bigl[10-t_{8,1-\alpha/2},\ 10+t_{8,1-\alpha/2}\bigr]\).
\end{example}

\begin{example}\label{r11-exm-confidence-ellipse}
Two dimensions of each of \(n=25\) machined parts are measured; the measurement vectors \(x_1,\dots,x_n\) are
modeled as independent \(N_2(\mu,\Sigma)\), with \(\Sigma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&3\\3&9\end{smallmatrix}\right)\)
known from long experience and \(\mu\) unknown. So the standard deviations are \(2\) and \(3\), and the
correlation is \(\frac12\).
\begin{itemize}
\item By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(d), \(u=(x_1',\dots,x_n')'\) is normal with covariance matrix
\(\diag(\Sigma,\dots,\Sigma)\), and \(\bar x=\frac1nGu\) with \(G=[I_2\ \cdots\ I_2]\). As in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{r11-prop-sample-moments}, Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a) gives
\(\bar x\sim N_2(\mu,\frac1n\Sigma)\). The standard deviations of \(\bar x_1\) and \(\bar x_2\) are
\(\frac25\) and \(\frac35\).
\item \(\Sigma\) is positive definite (minors \(4\), \(27\); Theorem~\ref{r8-thm-sylvester}). By
Corollary~\ref{r11-cor-chisq}(b), with \((\frac1n\Sigma)^{-1}=n\Sigma^{-1}\),
\(q(\mu)=n(\bar x-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(\bar x-\mu)\sim\chi^2_2\), so
\(\Prob\bigl(q(\mu)\leqslant\chi^2_{2,1-\alpha}\bigr)=1-\alpha\). Thus the ellipse
\(\{m\in\R^2:n(\bar x-m)'\Sigma^{-1}(\bar x-m)\leqslant2\log(1/\alpha)\}\), computed from the observations,
contains \(\mu\) with probability \(1-\alpha\) for every \(\mu\): a \textbf{confidence region} with level \(1-\alpha\).
\item Let \(\alpha=\frac1{20}\), and compare two possible target values \(m_1\), \(m_2\) with
\(\bar x-m_1=(\frac45,\frac65)'\) and \(\bar x-m_2=(\frac45,-\frac65)'\). Each coordinate of \(\bar x\) is two of its
standard deviations away from the corresponding coordinate of \(m_1\), and also of \(m_2\). With
\(\Sigma^{-1}=\frac1{27}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}9&-3\\-3&4\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) and \(d=5(\bar x-m)\),
\(q(m)=25(\bar x-m)'\Sigma^{-1}(\bar x-m)=d'\Sigma^{-1}d=\frac1{27}(9d_1^2-6d_1d_2+4d_2^2)\), where \(d=(4,6)'\) for
\(m_1\) and \(d=(4,-6)'\) for \(m_2\):
\[
q(m_1)=\frac{144-144+144}{27}=\frac{16}3,\qquad q(m_2)=\frac{144+144+144}{27}=16
\]
As \(e<3\), \(e^8<3^8=6561<8000=20^3\), so \(e^{8/3}<20\) and \(q(m_1)=\frac{16}3<2\log20\): \(m_1\) lies inside the
confidence ellipse. As \(e>2\), \(e^8>256>20\), so \(q(m_2)=16>2\log20\): \(m_2\) lies outside. The two
measurements are positively correlated, so deviations of the same sign are common and deviations of opposite
signs are unusual; only the joint region takes this into account.
\end{itemize}
\end{example}

\subsection*{Maximum likelihood for the multivariate normal}

\begin{definition}\label{r11-def-likelihood}
Let observations be modeled as a random vector \(x\) with a density \(f_\theta\) that depends on a parameter
\(\theta\) ranging over a set \(\Theta\). For the observed value of \(x\), the function
\(L(\theta)=f_\theta(x)\) (\(\theta\in\Theta\)) is the \textbf{likelihood}, \(\ell(\theta)=\log L(\theta)\) is the
\textbf{log-likelihood}, and a \(\hat\theta\in\Theta\) with \(L(\hat\theta)\geqslant L(\theta)\) for all
\(\theta\in\Theta\) is a \textbf{maximum likelihood estimate} (MLE) of \(\theta\).
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}\label{r11-thm-mle}
Let \(x_1,\dots,x_n\in\R^p\) be observations modeled as independent with distribution \(N_p(\mu,\Sigma)\),
where \(\mu\in\R^p\) and the symmetric positive definite \(\Sigma\) are unknown. Let
\[
\bar x=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^nx_i,\qquad S_n=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n(x_i-\bar x)(x_i-\bar x)',
\]
and suppose that \(S_n\) is positive definite (this requires \(n\geqslant p+1\),
Proposition~\ref{r8-prop-sample-cov}(b)).
\begin{enumerate}
\item The log-likelihood is
\[
\ell(\mu,\Sigma)=-\frac{np}2\log(2\pi)
-\frac n2\Bigl(\log\det\Sigma+\tr(\Sigma^{-1}S_n)+(\bar x-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(\bar x-\mu)\Bigr)
\]
\item \(\ell(\mu,\Sigma)\leqslant\ell(\bar x,S_n)=-\frac{np}2\log(2\pi)-\frac n2\log\det S_n-\frac{np}2\), with
equality only for \(\mu=\bar x\) and \(\Sigma=S_n\). As \(\log\) is strictly increasing, \((\bar x,S_n)\) is the
unique MLE of \((\mu,\Sigma)\).
\item Regarded as random vectors, \(\bar x\) and \(S_n\) satisfy \(\E\bar x=\mu\) and
\(\E S_n=\frac{n-1}n\Sigma\): the MLE of \(\Sigma\) is not unbiased, while \(S=\frac n{n-1}S_n\) is.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(a)
\begin{itemize}
\item By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-density} and (P4), the stacked vector \((x_1',\dots,x_n')'\) has the density
\(\prod_if(x_i)\), with \(f\) the \(N_p(\mu,\Sigma)\) density; so
\[
\ell(\mu,\Sigma)=-\frac{np}2\log(2\pi)-\frac n2\log\det\Sigma-\frac12\sum_{i=1}^n(x_i-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(x_i-\mu)
\]
\item Write \(x_i-\mu=(x_i-\bar x)+(\bar x-\mu)\). As \(\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)=0\), the cross terms add up to \(0\):
\[
\sum_i(x_i-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(x_i-\mu)=\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)'\Sigma^{-1}(x_i-\bar x)+n(\bar x-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(\bar x-\mu)
\]
\item \((x_i-\bar x)'\Sigma^{-1}(x_i-\bar x)=\tr\bigl(\Sigma^{-1}(x_i-\bar x)(x_i-\bar x)'\bigr)\)
(Theorem~\ref{r1-thm-trace}(b)), and the sum over \(i\) is \(\tr(\Sigma^{-1}nS_n)\).
\end{itemize}
(b)
\begin{itemize}
\item \(\Sigma^{-1}\) is positive definite (Corollary~\ref{r8-cor-pd}(a)), so the last term in (a) is
\(\geqslant0\), with equality only for \(\mu=\bar x\). It remains to show that
\(g(\Sigma)=\log\det\Sigma+\tr(\Sigma^{-1}S_n)\geqslant\log\det S_n+p\), with equality only for \(\Sigma=S_n\).
\item This is Problem~\ref{r9-pr-covariance-ml}(b),(c), with \(p\) in place of \(n\) and the positive definite
\(S=S_n\): its function \(\varphi(X)=\log\det X+\tr(X^{-1}S_n)\) has \(\varphi(\Sigma)=g(\Sigma)\) and
\(\varphi(S_n)=\log\det S_n+\tr I_p=\log\det S_n+p\).
\end{itemize}
(c) The \(x_i\) are independent, hence uncorrelated (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-moments}(e)), with mean \(\mu\) and
covariance matrix \(\Sigma\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-construct}). By
Proposition~\ref{r11-prop-sample-moments}, \(\E\bar x=\mu\) and \(\E S_n=\frac{n-1}n\E S=\frac{n-1}n\Sigma\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{r11-rem-mle-calculus}
On the open set \(U=\{X\in\R^{p\times p}:\det X>0\}\), the function \(g(X)=\log\det X+\tr(X^{-1}S_n)\) has
\(\partial g/\partial X=(X^{-1}-X^{-1}S_nX^{-1})'\) (Problem~\ref{r9-pr-covariance-ml}(a) with \(S=S_n\)). As
\(X^{-1}-X^{-1}S_nX^{-1}=X^{-1}(X-S_n)X^{-1}\), this derivative is \(0\) only at \(X=S_n\). A stationary point
alone does not show that \(S_n\) minimizes \(g\) over the positive definite matrices; the proof of
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mle}(b) does. Likewise, by Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mle}(a), \(\ell\) depends on \(\mu\) only
through
\[
-\frac n2(\bar x-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(\bar x-\mu)=-\frac n2\bigl(\mu'\Sigma^{-1}\mu-2\bar x'\Sigma^{-1}\mu
+\bar x'\Sigma^{-1}\bar x\bigr),
\]
whose gradient with respect to \(\mu\) is \(n\Sigma^{-1}(\bar x-\mu)\) (Theorem~\ref{r9-thm-linear-quadratic});
it vanishes only at \(\mu=\bar x\).
\end{remark}

\begin{example}\label{r11-exm-mle}
Model the four observations \((3,5,6)\), \((3,3,2)\), \((1,3,4)\), \((1,1,4)\) as independent from \(N_3(\mu,\Sigma)\)
(\(n=4\), \(p=3\)). Then \(\bar x=(2,3,4)'\), and the deviations \(x_i-\bar x\) are the rows of
\[
D=\begin{pmatrix}1&2&2\\1&0&-2\\-1&0&0\\-1&-2&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
nS_n=D'D=\begin{pmatrix}4&4&0\\4&8&4\\0&4&8\end{pmatrix},\qquad
S_n=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&0\\1&2&1\\0&1&2\end{pmatrix}
\]
Here \(S_n=LL'\) with \(L=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&0\\1&1&0\\0&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), so \(S_n\) is
positive definite (Theorem~\ref{r8-thm-cholesky}) and \(\det S_n=(\det L)^2=1\). By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mle}, the MLE
is \(\hat\mu=(2,3,4)'\), \(\hat\Sigma=S_n\), and the largest value of the log-likelihood is
\(-6\log(2\pi)-2\log1-6=-6(1+\log2\pi)\). The unbiased estimate of \(\Sigma\) is \(S=\frac43S_n\). For \(p=1\),
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mle} says that for independent observations from \(N(\mu,\sigma^2)\) the MLE is
\(\hat\mu=\bar x\), \(\hat\sigma^2=\frac1n\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)^2=\frac{n-1}ns^2\), when the \(x_i\) are not all equal.
\end{example}

\subsection*{Exercises}

\begin{exercise}\label{r11-ex-moments-drill}
Let \(x\in\R^4\) have \(\E x=\mu=(1,0,2,-1)'\) and
\[
\var(x)=\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}9&0&3&3\\0&1&2&1\\3&2&9&5\\3&1&5&4\end{pmatrix}
\]
\begin{enumerate}
\item Show that \(\Sigma=LL'\) for \(L=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}3&0&0&0\\0&1&0&0\\1&2&2&0\\1&1&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\),
and deduce that no linear combination \(a'x\) with \(a\ne0\) is constant with probability \(1\).
\item Find \(\E y\) and \(\var(y)\) for \(y=(x_1-x_2,\ x_3-x_4-3)'\).
\item Find \(\cov(x_1+x_3,\ x_2-x_4)\) and \(\corr(x)\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}
\begin{solution}
(a) The \((i,j)\) entry of \(LL'\) is \(\row_i(L)\row_j(L)'\) (Theorem~\ref{r1-thm-views}(d)). With the rows
\((3,0,0,0)\), \((0,1,0,0)\), \((1,2,2,0)\), \((1,1,1,1)\) this gives \(9,0,3,3\) in row \(1\); \(1,2,1\) at \((2,2)\),
\((2,3)\), \((2,4)\); \(1+4+4=9\) and \(1+2+2=5\) at \((3,3)\), \((3,4)\); and \(4\) at \((4,4)\). So \(LL'=\Sigma\). As \(L\)
is lower triangular with positive diagonal entries, \(\Sigma\) is positive definite
(Theorem~\ref{r8-thm-cholesky}), hence invertible (Corollary~\ref{r8-cor-pd}(a)), and by
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-cov-psd}(b) no \(a'x\) with \(a\ne0\) is constant with probability \(1\).

(b) \(y=Ax+b\) with \(A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-1&0&0\\0&0&1&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) and \(b=(0,-3)'\). By
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-moments}(a),(b), \(\E y=A\mu+b=(1-0,\ 2+1-3)'=(1,0)'\), and the rows of \(A\Sigma\) are
\(\row_1(\Sigma)-\row_2(\Sigma)\) and \(\row_3(\Sigma)-\row_4(\Sigma)\):
\[
A\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}9&-1&1&2\\0&1&4&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\var(y)=A\Sigma A'=\begin{pmatrix}10&-1\\-1&3\end{pmatrix}
\]

(c) \(\Sigma(0,1,0,-1)'=\col_2(\Sigma)-\col_4(\Sigma)=(-3,0,-3,-3)'\), so by Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-moments}(b)
\(\cov(x_1+x_3,\ x_2-x_4)=(1,0,1,0)(-3,0,-3,-3)'=-6\). The standard deviations are \(3,1,3,2\), so
\(D^{-1/2}=\diag(\frac13,1,\frac13,\frac12)\) and
\[
\corr(x)=D^{-1/2}\Sigma D^{-1/2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&1/3&1/2\\0&1&2/3&1/2\\1/3&2/3&1&5/6\\1/2&1/2&5/6&1\end{pmatrix}
\]
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{r11-ex-quadratic-blp}
Let \(x\) be as in Exercise~\ref{r11-ex-moments-drill}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Use Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-quadratic-mean} to find \(\E(x_1x_3+x_2^2)\) and \(\E\lVert x\rVert^2\), and check
the first value directly from the means, variances and covariances.
\item Find the best linear predictor of \(x_1\) from \((x_2,x_3,x_4)'\) and its mean squared error. Compare with the
best linear predictors from \(x_2\) alone and from \(x_4\) alone.
\item Check directly that the prediction error \(u\) of (b) has mean \(0\), that
\(\cov(u,x_2)=\cov(u,x_3)=\cov(u,x_4)=0\), and that \(\var(u)\) is the mean squared error.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}
\begin{solution}
(a) \(x_1x_3+x_2^2=x'Ax\) with \(A=e_1e_3'+e_2e_2'\). The rows of \(A\Sigma\) are \(\row_3(\Sigma)\), \(\row_2(\Sigma)\),
\(0\), \(0\), so \(\tr(A\Sigma)=\sigma_{31}+\sigma_{22}=3+1=4\); and \(\mu'A\mu=\mu_1\mu_3+\mu_2^2=2\). Hence
\(\E(x_1x_3+x_2^2)=4+2=6\). Directly, by Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-moments}(c),
\(\E(x_1x_3)=\cov(x_1,x_3)+\mu_1\mu_3=3+2=5\) and \(\E x_2^2=\var(x_2)+\mu_2^2=1\), with sum \(6\). With \(A=I_4\),
\(\E\lVert x\rVert^2=\tr\Sigma+\mu'\mu=23+6=29\).

(b) In Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-blp} take \(y=x_1\) and \((x_2,x_3,x_4)'\) as the predictors:
\[
\Sigma_{xx}=\begin{pmatrix}1&2&1\\2&9&5\\1&5&4\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma_{xy}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\3\\3\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\Sigma_{xx}\begin{pmatrix}-1\\0\\1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\3\\3\end{pmatrix}
\]
\(\Sigma_{xx}\) is a principal submatrix of the positive definite \(\Sigma\), so it is positive definite and invertible
(Corollary~\ref{r8-cor-pd}(a)), and \(b^*=\Sigma_{xx}^{-1}\sigma_{xy}=(-1,0,1)'\). Then
\(a^*=\mu_1-b^{*\prime}(\mu_2,\mu_3,\mu_4)'=1-(0+0-1)=2\), the best linear predictor is \(2-x_2+x_4\), and its mean
squared error is \(9-(0,3,3)(-1,0,1)'=6\). From \(x_4\) alone the minimum is \(9-3^2/4=\frac{27}4\). As
\(\cov(x_1,x_2)=0\), the best linear predictor from \(x_2\) alone is the constant \(\mu_1=1\), with mean squared
error \(\var(x_1)=9\): alone, \(x_2\) is useless, but together with \(x_4\) it receives the coefficient \(-1\).

(c) \(u=x_1-2+x_2-x_4=c'x-2\) with \(c=(1,1,0,-1)'\). Then \(\E u=c'\mu-2=1+0+0+1-2=0\), and
\(\Sigma c=\col_1(\Sigma)+\col_2(\Sigma)-\col_4(\Sigma)=(6,0,0,0)'\). By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-moments}(b),
\(\cov(u,x_j)=c'\Sigma e_j\) is entry \(j\) of \(\Sigma c\), which is \(0\) for \(j=2,3,4\), and
\(\var(u)=c'\Sigma c=6\).
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{r11-ex-normal-drill}
Let \(x\sim N_4(\mu,\Sigma)\) with \(\mu=(1,2,0,-1)'\) and
\[
\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}3&1&1&0\\1&3&1&1\\1&1&2&1\\0&1&1&1\end{pmatrix}
\]
\begin{enumerate}
\item Find the distributions of \(y=(x_1-x_2,\ x_3-x_4)'\) and of \((x_1,x_4)'\).
\item Which of the six pairs \(\{x_i,x_j\}\) are independent? Is \(x_1\) independent of \((x_3,x_4)'\)?
\item Find \(c\) such that \(x_1-cx_3\) and \(x_3\) are independent.
\item Find the conditional distribution of \((x_1,x_2)'\) given \((x_3,x_4)'=(a_3,a_4)'\), and check that
\(\det\Sigma=\det\Sigma_{22}\det\Sigma_{11\cdot2}\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}
\begin{solution}
(a) \(y=Ax\) with \(A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-1&0&0\\0&0&1&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\). The rows of \(A\Sigma\) are
\(\row_1(\Sigma)-\row_2(\Sigma)=(2,-2,0,-1)\) and \(\row_3(\Sigma)-\row_4(\Sigma)=(1,0,1,0)\), so by
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a)
\[
y\sim N_2\left(\begin{pmatrix}-1\\1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}4&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}\right),\qquad
\begin{pmatrix}x_1\\x_4\end{pmatrix}\sim N_2\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\-1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}3&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\right),
\]
the second by Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(b).

(b) Each pair forms a normal subvector of \(x\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(b)), so
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(a) applies to it: \(x_1\indep x_4\), as \(\sigma_{14}=0\), and the other five
pairs are not independent, as their covariances are not \(0\). The vector \((x_1,x_3,x_4)'\) is normal, and the
cross-covariance of \(x_1\) and \((x_3,x_4)'\) is \((\sigma_{13},\sigma_{14})=(1,0)\ne0\); so \(x_1\) is not independent
of \((x_3,x_4)'\).

(c) By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(b) with \(A=(1,0,-c,0)\) and \(B=e_3'\),
\(A\Sigma B'=\sigma_{13}-c\sigma_{33}=1-2c\), which is \(0\) exactly for \(c=\frac12\).

(d) In Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-conditional}, the first block is \((x_1,x_2)'\) and the second \((x_3,x_4)'\):
\[
\Sigma_{22}=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\Sigma_{22}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\-1&2\end{pmatrix},\quad
\Sigma_{12}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix},\quad B=\Sigma_{12}\Sigma_{22}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\0&1\end{pmatrix},
\]
with \(\Sigma_{22}^{-1}\) from Proposition~\ref{r2-prop-inverse-2x2} (\(\det\Sigma_{22}=1\)), and
\[
\Sigma_{11\cdot2}=\Sigma_{11}-B\Sigma_{21}=\begin{pmatrix}3&1\\1&3\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}
\]
The conditional mean is \((1,2)'+B(a_3-0,\ a_4+1)'=(a_3-a_4,\ a_4+3)'\), so
\((x_1,x_2)'\mid(x_3,x_4)'=(a_3,a_4)'\sim N_2\bigl((a_3-a_4,\ a_4+3)',\ \Sigma_{11\cdot2}\bigr)\). Check: the leading principal
minors of \(\Sigma\) are \(3\), \(8\), \(12\) and \(\det\Sigma=3\), and \(\det\Sigma_{22}\det\Sigma_{11\cdot2}=1\cdot3\)
(Theorem~\ref{r5d-thm-schur-det}(b)).
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{r11-ex-density-drill}
A random vector \(x\in\R^2\) has the density
\[
f(u)=\frac1{2\pi}\exp\Bigl(-\frac12\bigl(u_1^2-2u_1u_2+2u_2^2-2u_2+1\bigr)\Bigr)
\]
Show that \(x\) is bivariate normal, and find \(\mu\), \(\Sigma\), \(\corr(x_1,x_2)\) and the conditional distribution
of \(x_1\) given \(x_2=a\).
\end{exercise}
\begin{solution}
Let \(P=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-1\\-1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) and \(\mu=(1,1)'\). Then
\(u'Pu=u_1^2-2u_1u_2+2u_2^2\), \(P\mu=(0,1)'\) and \(\mu'P\mu=1\), so
\[
(u-\mu)'P(u-\mu)=u'Pu-2(P\mu)'u+\mu'P\mu=u_1^2-2u_1u_2+2u_2^2-2u_2+1
\]
The matrix \(\Sigma=P^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1\\1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) (Proposition~\ref{r2-prop-inverse-2x2})
is positive definite (minors \(2\), \(1\); Theorem~\ref{r8-thm-sylvester}), and \(\det\Sigma=1\). So \(f\) is the
\(N_2(\mu,\Sigma)\) density of Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-density}. Hence \(\Prob(x\in A)=\int_Af=\Prob(y\in A)\) for
\(y\sim N_2(\mu,\Sigma)\) and all \(A\): \(x\sim N_2\bigl((1,1)',\Sigma\bigr)\). Then
\(\corr(x_1,x_2)=1/\sqrt{2\cdot1}=1/\sqrt2\). By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-conditional},
\(B=\sigma_{12}/\sigma_{22}=1\) and \(\Sigma_{11\cdot2}=2-1=1\), so \(x_1\mid x_2=a\sim N(1+(a-1),\ 1)=N(a,1)\).
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{r11-ex-simulation}
A program produces independent \(N(0,1)\) numbers.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Let \(L\in\R^{4\times4}\) have \(l_{ij}=1\) for \(j\leqslant i\) and \(l_{ij}=0\) for \(j>i\). Show that \(L\) is the
Cholesky factor of \(\Sigma=(\min(i,j))_{i,j\leqslant4}\), and that \(x=\mu+Lz\sim N_4(\mu,\Sigma)\) when \(z\)
consists of four of the numbers. Describe \(x_k-\mu_k\) in terms of \(z\).
\item Compute \(x\) for \(\mu=(1,0,-1,0)'\) and \(z=(1,-2,2,1)'\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}
\begin{solution}
(a) \(L\) is lower triangular with positive diagonal entries. The \((i,j)\) entry of \(LL'\) is
\(\row_i(L)\row_j(L)'\) (Theorem~\ref{r1-thm-views}(d)); \(\row_i(L)\) has ones in positions \(1,\dots,i\), so this
product counts the positions \(k\leqslant\min(i,j)\) and equals \(\min(i,j)\). So
\[
LL'=\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1&1\\1&2&2&2\\1&2&3&3\\1&2&3&4\end{pmatrix},
\]
and by Theorem~\ref{r8-thm-cholesky} \(L\) is the Cholesky factor of \(\Sigma\); Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-construct}
gives \(\mu+Lz\sim N_4(\mu,LL')\). Here \(x_k-\mu_k=z_1+\dots+z_k\): the partial sums of the numbers, a random
walk with \(N(0,1)\) steps.

(b) \(Lz=(1,\ 1-2,\ 1-2+2,\ 1-2+2+1)'=(1,-1,1,2)'\), so \(x=(2,-1,0,2)'\).
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{r11-ex-partial-correlation}
Let \(x\sim N_4(\mu,\Sigma)\) with
\[
\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}4&1&2&1\\1&4&2&1\\2&2&2&1\\1&1&1&1\end{pmatrix}
\]
The correlation computed from the conditional covariance matrix of \((x_1,x_2)'\) given some of the other
variables is the \textbf{partial correlation} of \(x_1\) and \(x_2\) given those variables.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Show that \(\Sigma\) is positive definite. Find the conditional distribution of \((x_1,x_2)'\) given
\((x_3,x_4)'=(a_3,a_4)'\), and compare the partial correlation of \(x_1\) and \(x_2\) given \(x_3,x_4\) with \(\corr(x_1,x_2)\).
\item Find the partial correlation of \(x_1\) and \(x_2\) given \(x_3\) alone, and given \(x_4\) alone.
\item Let \(u=(x_1,x_2)'-B(x_3,x_4)'\), with \(B\) as in (a). Compute \(\var(u)\) from
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-moments}(b), and compare with (a).
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}
\begin{solution}
(a) The leading principal minors of \(\Sigma\) are \(4\), \(15\), \(6\) and \(\det\Sigma=3\), all positive
(Theorem~\ref{r8-thm-sylvester}). In Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-conditional} with first block \((x_1,x_2)'\) and second
block \((x_3,x_4)'\): \(\Sigma_{22}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1\\1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\),
\(\Sigma_{22}^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-1\\-1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) (Proposition~\ref{r2-prop-inverse-2x2}),
\(\Sigma_{12}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1\\2&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) and
\[
B=\Sigma_{12}\Sigma_{22}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\Sigma_{11\cdot2}=\begin{pmatrix}4&1\\1&4\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}2&2\\1&1\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\-1&2\end{pmatrix}
\]
So \((x_1,x_2)'\mid(x_3,x_4)'=(a_3,a_4)'\sim N_2\bigl((\mu_1,\mu_2)'+(a_3-\mu_3)(1,1)',\ \Sigma_{11\cdot2}\bigr)\). The partial
correlation is \(-1/\sqrt{2\cdot2}=-\frac12\), while \(\corr(x_1,x_2)=1/\sqrt{4\cdot4}=\frac14\): the sign changes.

(b) Given \(x_3\): \(\Sigma_{11}-\frac12(2,2)'(2,2)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&1\\1&4\end{smallmatrix}\right)
-\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&2\\2&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&-1\\-1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), partial
correlation \(-\frac12\). Given \(x_4\): \(\Sigma_{11}-(1,1)'(1,1)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}3&0\\0&3\end{smallmatrix}\right)\),
partial correlation \(0\).

(c) \(u=(x_1-x_3,\ x_2-x_3)'=Kx\) with \(K=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&-1&0\\0&1&-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\). The rows
of \(K\Sigma\) are \((2,-1,0,0)\) and \((-1,2,0,0)\), so
\(\var(u)=K\Sigma K'=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&-1\\-1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\Sigma_{11\cdot2}\). This used only
the covariance matrix: \(u_1\) and \(u_2\) are the prediction errors of the best linear predictors of \(x_1\) and
\(x_2\) from \((x_3,x_4)'\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-blp}), and the partial correlation is their correlation.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{r11-ex-projection-drill}
Let \(y\sim N_4(\mu,\sigma^2I_4)\) with \(\sigma>0\), and
\[
P=\frac12\begin{pmatrix}1&1&0&0\\1&1&0&0\\0&0&1&1\\0&0&1&1\end{pmatrix}
\]
\begin{enumerate}
\item Show that \(P\) and \(I_4-P\) are symmetric and idempotent of rank \(2\), that
\(y'Py=\frac12(y_1+y_2)^2+\frac12(y_3+y_4)^2\), and that \(y'(I_4-P)y=\frac12(y_1-y_2)^2+\frac12(y_3-y_4)^2\).
\item Find \(\E(y'Py)\) and \(\E\bigl(y'(I_4-P)y\bigr)\) for \(\mu=(1,3,5,7)'\).
\item Let \(\mu=(m_1,m_1,m_2,m_2)'\). Show that \(y'(I_4-P)y/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2_2\), that \(y'(I_4-P)y\) is
independent of \(Py\), and that \(\frac12y'(I_4-P)y\) is an unbiased estimator of \(\sigma^2\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}
\begin{solution}
(a) \(P=\frac12\diag(J_2,J_2)\) is symmetric, and \(J_2^2=2J_2\) (Proposition~\ref{r1-prop-centering}), so
\(P^2=\frac14\diag(J_2^2,J_2^2)=\frac12\diag(J_2,J_2)=P\) (Theorem~\ref{r5b-thm-block}(c)). By Theorem~\ref{r6-thm-idempotent}(b),(c), \(I_4-P\) is idempotent, \(\rank P=\tr P=2\)
and \(\rank(I_4-P)=\tr(I_4-P)=2\). As \(Py=\frac12(y_1+y_2,\ y_1+y_2,\ y_3+y_4,\ y_3+y_4)'\),
\(y'Py=\frac12(y_1+y_2)^2+\frac12(y_3+y_4)^2\); and \(y'(I_4-P)y=y'y-y'Py\), where
\(y_1^2+y_2^2-\frac12(y_1+y_2)^2=\frac12(y_1-y_2)^2\), and likewise for \(y_3,y_4\).

(b) By Example~\ref{r11-exm-quadratic-projection} with \(r=2\),
\(\E(y'Py)=2\sigma^2+\mu'P\mu=2\sigma^2+\frac12\cdot4^2+\frac12\cdot12^2=2\sigma^2+80\) and
\(\E\bigl(y'(I_4-P)y\bigr)=2\sigma^2+\frac12\cdot2^2+\frac12\cdot2^2=2\sigma^2+4\).

(c) Let \(\varepsilon=y-\mu\sim N_4(0,\sigma^2I_4)\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a)). Here \((I_4-P)\mu=0\), so
\((I_4-P)y=(I_4-P)\varepsilon\) and, as \(I_4-P\) is symmetric and idempotent,
\(y'(I_4-P)y=\lVert(I_4-P)y\rVert^2=\varepsilon'(I_4-P)\varepsilon\). Corollary~\ref{r11-cor-chisq}(a) gives
\(y'(I_4-P)y/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2_2\). As \(P(I_4-P)=P-P^2=0\), Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-quadratic-independence}(c) gives
\(P\varepsilon\indep\varepsilon'(I_4-P)\varepsilon\), and \(Py=P\mu+P\varepsilon\) is a function of \(P\varepsilon\)
(P1). \(\E\bigl(y'(I_4-P)y\bigr)=2\sigma^2+0\) (Example~\ref{r11-exm-quadratic-projection}), so
\(\frac12y'(I_4-P)y\) is unbiased for \(\sigma^2\) for all values of \(m_1,m_2,\sigma^2\)
(Definition~\ref{r11-def-estimator}).
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{r11-ex-helmert}
Let \(z\sim N_4(0,\sigma^2I_4)\) with \(\sigma>0\), and
\[
q_1=\tfrac14(z_1+z_2+z_3+z_4)^2,\quad q_2=\tfrac12(z_1-z_2)^2,\quad q_3=\tfrac16(z_1+z_2-2z_3)^2,\quad
q_4=\tfrac1{12}(z_1+z_2+z_3-3z_4)^2
\]
\begin{enumerate}
\item Write \(q_i=z'A_iz\) with \(A_i\) symmetric of rank \(1\), and show that \(A_1+A_2+A_3+A_4=I_4\).
\item Show that \(q_1/\sigma^2,\dots,q_4/\sigma^2\) are independent with distribution \(\chi^2_1\).
\item Show that \(q_2+q_3+q_4=\sum_i(z_i-\bar z)^2\), that it is independent of \(\bar z\), and that
\((q_2+q_3+q_4)/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2_3\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}
\begin{solution}
(a) Let \(a_1=\one_4\), \(a_2=(1,-1,0,0)'\), \(a_3=(1,1,-2,0)'\) and \(a_4=(1,1,1,-3)'\), with \(a_i'a_i=4,2,6,12\). Then
\(q_i=(a_i'z)^2/(a_i'a_i)=z'A_iz\) for \(A_i=a_ia_i'/(a_i'a_i)\), which is symmetric of rank \(1\) (its columns are
multiples of \(a_i\ne0\), not all \(0\)). The six products \(a_i'a_j\) (\(i<j\)) are \(0\): for instance
\(a_3'a_4=1+1-2+0=0\). So the vectors \(u_i=a_i/\sqrt{a_i'a_i}\) are orthonormal, \(U=[u_1\ \cdots\ u_4]\) satisfies \(U'U=I_4\), and
\(UU'=I_4\) (Proposition~\ref{r6-prop-orthonormal}(b)). By the outer product expansion
(Theorem~\ref{r1-thm-views}(d)), \(UU'=\sum_iu_iu_i'=\sum_iA_i\).

(b) The ranks add up to \(4\); apply Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-cochran}(b).

(c) \(A_2+A_3+A_4=I_4-A_1=I_4-\bar J_4=C_4\), so \(q_2+q_3+q_4=z'C_4z=\sum_i(z_i-\bar z)^2\)
(Example~\ref{r1-exm-centering}). As \(\bar z=\frac14\one'z\) and \(\frac14\one'C_4=0\),
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-quadratic-independence}(c) gives \(\bar z\indep z'C_4z\). \(C_4\) is symmetric and
idempotent of rank \(3\) (Proposition~\ref{r1-prop-centering}, Corollary~\ref{r6-cor-centering}(b)), so
\((q_2+q_3+q_4)/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2_3\) by Corollary~\ref{r11-cor-chisq}(a).
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{r11-ex-ellipse}
Let \(x\sim N_2(\mu,\Sigma)\) with \(\mu=(1,1)'\) and
\(\Sigma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}5&3\\3&5\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), and let \(d^2=(x-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(x-\mu)\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item Show that \(\Prob(d^2\leqslant c)=1-e^{-c/2}\) (\(c\geqslant0\)), and find \(c\) with \(\Prob(d^2\leqslant c)=1-\alpha\).
\item Describe the ellipse \(E_\alpha=\{u:(u-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(u-\mu)\leqslant c\}\) for this \(c\).
\item Which of the points \((4,-2)\) and \((5,5)\) lie in \(E_\alpha\) for \(\alpha=\frac1{20}\)? For each, find the
probability that \(d^2\) exceeds its value of \((u-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(u-\mu)\), and compare with the Euclidean distances
from \(\mu\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}
\begin{solution}
(a) \(\Sigma\) is positive definite (minors \(5\), \(16\); Theorem~\ref{r8-thm-sylvester}), so \(d^2\sim\chi^2_2\)
(Corollary~\ref{r11-cor-chisq}(b)), and \(\Prob(d^2\leqslant c)=1-e^{-c/2}\) by (P5). This is \(1-\alpha\) for
\(c=2\log(1/\alpha)\).

(b) \(\Sigma(1,1)'=8(1,1)'\) and \(\Sigma(1,-1)'=2(1,-1)'\): the eigenvalues are \(8\) and \(2\), with unit eigenvectors
\(\frac1{\sqrt2}(1,1)'\) and \(\frac1{\sqrt2}(1,-1)'\). By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-density}, \(E_\alpha\) has center
\((1,1)\), semi-axis \(\sqrt{8c}=4\sqrt{\log(1/\alpha)}\) along \((1,1)'\), and semi-axis \(\sqrt{2c}=2\sqrt{\log(1/\alpha)}\)
along \((1,-1)'\).

(c) \(\det\Sigma=16\) and \(\Sigma^{-1}=\frac1{16}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}5&-3\\-3&5\end{smallmatrix}\right)\). For
\((4,-2)\), \(u-\mu=(3,-3)'\) and \((u-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(u-\mu)=\frac1{16}(45+54+45)=9\). As \(e>2\),
\(e^{9/2}>2^{9/2}=16\sqrt2>20\) (since \(512>400\)), so \(9>2\log20\): the point lies outside \(E_{1/20}\), and
\(\Prob(d^2>9)=e^{-9/2}\). For \((5,5)\), \(u-\mu=(4,4)'\) and the value is \(\frac1{16}(80-96+80)=4\). As \(e<3\),
\(e^2<9<20\), so \(4<2\log20\): the point lies inside, and \(\Prob(d^2>4)=e^{-2}\). The Euclidean distances are
\(3\sqrt2\) and \(4\sqrt2\): the point farther from \(\mu\) is the less unusual one, because it lies in the direction
\((1,1)'\) of the large variance.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{r11-ex-variance-interval}
Three measurements \(4,7,10\) are modeled as independent \(N(\mu,\sigma^2)\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item Write down \(\bar x\), \((n-1)s^2\) and the interval of Example~\ref{r11-exm-t-interval} for \(\sigma^2\).
\item Use (P5) to show that \(\chi^2_{2,\tau}=-2\log(1-\tau)\) (\(0<\tau<1\)), and write the interval of (a) in
closed form. Does it contain \(1\) for \(\alpha=\frac1{20}\)?
\item Write down the interval of Example~\ref{r11-exm-t-interval} for \(\mu\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}
\begin{solution}
(a) Here \(n=3\), \(\bar x=7\) and \((n-1)s^2=9+0+9=18\). By Example~\ref{r11-exm-t-interval} the interval is
\(\bigl[18/\chi^2_{2,1-\alpha/2},\ 18/\chi^2_{2,\alpha/2}\bigr]\).

(b) By (P5), \(\Prob(v\leqslant c)=1-e^{-c/2}\) for \(v\sim\chi^2_2\), and \(1-e^{-c/2}=\tau\) exactly for
\(c=-2\log(1-\tau)\). So \(\chi^2_{2,1-\alpha/2}=2\log(2/\alpha)\) and
\(\chi^2_{2,\alpha/2}=2\log\bigl(2/(2-\alpha)\bigr)\), so the interval is
\[
\Bigl[\frac9{\log(2/\alpha)},\ \frac9{\log\bigl(2/(2-\alpha)\bigr)}\Bigr],\qquad\text{for }\alpha=\tfrac1{20}:\quad
\Bigl[\frac9{\log40},\ \frac9{\log(40/39)}\Bigr]
\]
As \(e>2\), \(e^9>512>40\), so \(\log40<9\) and the lower endpoint exceeds \(1\): the interval does not contain \(1\). By
Problem~\ref{r9-pr-covariance-ml}(c) with \(\theta=\frac{40}{39}\), \(\log\frac{40}{39}<\frac{40}{39}-1=\frac1{39}\), so the
upper endpoint exceeds \(9\cdot39=351\). Three observations say little about \(\sigma^2\).

(c) \(s=3\) and \(s/\sqrt3=\sqrt3\), so the interval is
\(\bigl[7-\sqrt3\,t_{2,1-\alpha/2},\ 7+\sqrt3\,t_{2,1-\alpha/2}\bigr]\).
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{r11-ex-mle-drill}
The five observations \((5,3)\), \((4,4)\), \((3,5)\), \((2,1)\), \((1,2)\) are modeled as independent from
\(N_2(\mu,\Sigma)\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item Find the MLE \((\hat\mu,\hat\Sigma)\), the largest value of the log-likelihood, and the correlation computed
from \(\hat\Sigma\).
\item Compute \(\ell(\bar x,S)\) for the unbiased estimate \(S\), and check that it is smaller than
\(\ell(\bar x,S_n)\).
\item Find the eigenvalues \(\theta_j\) of \(C=S_n^{1/2}S^{-1}S_n^{1/2}\), and check the identity
\(\varphi(S)-\varphi(S_n)=\sum_j(\theta_j-1-\log\theta_j)\) of Problem~\ref{r9-pr-covariance-ml}(b) for
\(\varphi(X)=\log\det X+\tr(X^{-1}S_n)\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}
\begin{solution}
(a) \(\bar x=(3,3)'\), and the deviations \(x_i-\bar x\) are \((2,0)\), \((1,1)\), \((0,2)\), \((-1,-2)\), \((-2,-1)\). So
\[
nS_n=\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)(x_i-\bar x)'=\begin{pmatrix}10&5\\5&10\end{pmatrix},\qquad
S_n=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix},\qquad\det S_n=3>0
\]
(for instance \(5=0+1+0+2+2\)). As \(S_n\) is positive definite (Theorem~\ref{r8-thm-sylvester}),
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mle} gives \(\hat\mu=(3,3)'\), \(\hat\Sigma=S_n\), and the largest log-likelihood
\(-5\log(2\pi)-\frac52\log3-5\). The correlation computed from \(\hat\Sigma\) is \(1/\sqrt{2\cdot2}=\frac12\).

(b) \(S=\frac54S_n\), \(\det S=\frac{25}{16}\cdot3=\frac{75}{16}\), and \(\tr(S^{-1}S_n)=\tr(\frac45I_2)=\frac85\). By
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mle}(a), \(\ell(\bar x,S)=-5\log(2\pi)-\frac52\bigl(\log\frac{75}{16}+\frac85\bigr)\). Hence
\[
\ell(\bar x,S_n)-\ell(\bar x,S)=\frac52\Bigl(\log\frac{75}{16}+\frac85-\log3-2\Bigr)=\frac52\Bigl(\log\frac{25}{16}-\frac25\Bigr)
=5\log\frac54-1
\]
This is positive: \((\frac54)^5=\frac{3125}{1024}>3>e\), so \(5\log\frac54>1\).

(c) \(S^{-1}=\frac45S_n^{-1}\) and \(S_n^{-1}=S_n^{-1/2}S_n^{-1/2}\) with \(S_n^{-1/2}=(S_n^{1/2})^{-1}\)
(Theorem~\ref{r8-thm-sqrt}(c)), so \(C=\frac45S_n^{1/2}S_n^{-1}S_n^{1/2}=\frac45I_2\) and \(\theta_1=\theta_2=\frac45\). Then
\(\sum_j(\theta_j-1-\log\theta_j)=2\log\frac54-\frac25\), and
\(\varphi(S)-\varphi(S_n)=\log\frac{75}{16}+\frac85-\log3-2=2\log\frac54-\frac25\), the same number; it is
\(\frac25\bigl(\ell(\bar x,S_n)-\ell(\bar x,S)\bigr)\), as Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mle}(a) predicts.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{r11-ex-galton}
Galton related the heights of adult children to the \emph{mid-parent height}, the average height of the two
parents. Model the mid-parent height \(x\) and the child's height \(y\), in centimeters, as bivariate normal with
means \(\mu_x=\mu_y=170\), standard deviations \(\sigma_x=4\), \(\sigma_y=5\) and correlation \(\rho=\frac35\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item Find the conditional distribution of a child's height given mid-parent height \(178\), and given \(162\).
\item Express the conditional means of (a) in standard units, and explain the regression to the mean.
\item Find an interval \([L,U]\), computed from \(a\), with \(\Prob(L\leqslant y\leqslant U)=1-\alpha\)
under the conditional distribution of \(y\) given \(x=a\), for \(a=178\).
\item Find \(\E(x\mid y=180)\). Do tall children have parents as extreme as themselves?
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}
\begin{solution}
(a) By Example~\ref{r11-exm-bivariate}, the line of conditional means of \(y\) given \(x\) has slope
\(\rho\sigma_y/\sigma_x=\frac35\cdot\frac54=\frac34\), and the conditional variance is
\(\sigma_y^2(1-\rho^2)=25\cdot\frac{16}{25}=16\). The conditional means are \(170+\frac34(178-170)=176\) and
\(170+\frac34(162-170)=164\). So \(y\mid x=178\sim N(176,16)\) and \(y\mid x=162\sim N(164,16)\).

(b) \(178\) is \(8/4=2\) standard deviations above \(\mu_x\), and \(176\) is \(6/5\) standard deviations above \(\mu_y\):
\(\frac65=\frac35\cdot2\). Likewise \(162\) is \(2\) standard deviations below \(\mu_x\), and \(164\) is \(\frac65\) below
\(\mu_y\). The children of tall parents are tall, but less extreme than their parents in standard units, and the
children of short parents are short, but less so; this is the regression to the mean of
Example~\ref{r11-exm-bivariate}, since \(\rho<1\).

(c) Given \(x=178\), \(y\sim N(176,16)\), so \(w=(y-176)/4\sim N(0,1)\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a)). Let
\(c=z_{1-\alpha/2}\). As in Example~\ref{r11-exm-t-interval}, with \(w\) in place of \(T\) (\(-w\sim N(0,1)\)),
\(\Prob(-c\leqslant w\leqslant c)=1-\alpha\). Hence \(\bigl[176-4z_{1-\alpha/2},\ 176+4z_{1-\alpha/2}\bigr]\) is a prediction
interval with level \(1-\alpha\).

(d) Exchanging the roles of \(x\) and \(y\) (Example~\ref{r11-exm-bivariate}), the slope is
\(\rho\sigma_x/\sigma_y=\frac35\cdot\frac45=\frac{12}{25}\), and \(\E(x\mid y=180)=170+\frac{12}{25}\cdot10=170+\frac{24}5\).
The child is \(2\) standard deviations above \(\mu_y\), but the expected mid-parent height only
\(\frac{24}5/4=\frac65\) above \(\mu_x\). So regression to the mean also works backwards: it is a consequence of
\(\lvert\rho\rvert<1\), not a force acting from one generation to the next.
\end{solution}

\subsection*{Problems}

\begin{problem}\label{r11-pr-unbiased-s}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Let \(x\in\R^n\) have mean \(\mu\) and positive definite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\) (\(x\) need not be
normal). Show that \(\E\bigl((x-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(x-\mu)\bigr)=n\).
\item Let \(x_1,\dots,x_n\in\R^k\) satisfy the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{r11-prop-sample-moments}, with
sample covariance matrix \(S=(s_{jl})\). Apply Example~\ref{r11-exm-quadratic-projection} to the numbers
\(a'x_1,\dots,a'x_n\) for suitable \(a\in\R^k\) to give a second proof of \(\E S=\Sigma\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
(a) \(x-\mu\) has mean \(0\) and covariance matrix \(\Sigma\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-moments}(a),(b)). By
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-quadratic-mean} with \(A=\Sigma^{-1}\),
\(\E\bigl((x-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(x-\mu)\bigr)=\tr(\Sigma^{-1}\Sigma)+0=\tr I_n=n\).

(b) For \(a\in\R^k\) let \(y=(a'x_1,\dots,a'x_n)'\). By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-moments}(a),(b), \(\E(a'x_i)=a'\mu\),
\(\var(a'x_i)=a'\Sigma a\), and \(\cov(a'x_i,a'x_j)=a'\cov(x_i,x_j)a=0\) for \(i\ne j\); so \(\E y=(a'\mu)\one\) and
\(\var(y)=(a'\Sigma a)I_n\). Its sum of squares about the mean is
\(\sum_i(a'x_i-a'\bar x)^2=a'\bigl(\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)(x_i-\bar x)'\bigr)a=(n-1)a'Sa\). By the common-mean case of
Example~\ref{r11-exm-quadratic-projection}, \((n-1)\E(a'Sa)=(n-1)a'\Sigma a\), that is, \(a'(\E S)a=a'\Sigma a\)
(Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-moments}(a)). With \(a=e_j\) this gives \(\E s_{jj}=\sigma_{jj}\). With \(a=e_j+e_l\) and
\(a=e_j-e_l\) (\(j\ne l\)), as \(S\) and \(\Sigma\) are symmetric,
\(\E s_{jj}\pm2\E s_{jl}+\E s_{ll}=\sigma_{jj}\pm2\sigma_{jl}+\sigma_{ll}\); subtracting the two equations gives
\(\E s_{jl}=\sigma_{jl}\).
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{r11-pr-singular-normal}
Let \(x\sim N_n(\mu,\Sigma)\) with \(\rank\Sigma=r\), \(1\leqslant r<n\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item Show that \(\Sigma=CC'\) for some \(C\in\R^{n\times r}\) of rank \(r\), and that
\(w=(C'C)^{-1}C'(x-\mu)\sim N_r(0,I_r)\).
\item Let \(d=x-\mu-Cw\). Show that \(\E d=0\) and \(\var(d)=0\), and deduce that \(x=\mu+Cw\) with probability
\(1\). (Consider \(Y=\lVert d\rVert\).)
\item For \(x\) as in Example~\ref{r11-exm-singular} with \(u\sim N_2(0,I_2)\), find \(C\) and \(w\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
(a) By Theorem~\ref{r8-thm-gram}(c), \(\Sigma=B'B\) with \(B\in\R^{r\times n}\) of rank \(r\); take \(C=B'\), of rank
\(r\) (Theorem~\ref{thm:r4-rank-rules}(a)). Then \(C'C\in\R^{r\times r}\) has rank \(r\)
(Theorem~\ref{thm:r4-rank-ata}(b)), so it is invertible. With \(K=(C'C)^{-1}C'\), \(w=K(x-\mu)\) is normal with
mean \(0\) and covariance matrix
\(K\Sigma K'=(C'C)^{-1}C'CC'C(C'C)^{-1}=I_r\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a)).

(b) \(d=(I_n-CK)(x-\mu)\), so \(\E d=0\) and \(\var(d)=(I_n-CK)CC'(I_n-CK)'=0\), since
\((I_n-CK)C=C-C(C'C)^{-1}C'C=0\). By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-quadratic-mean},
\(\E Y^2=\E(d'd)=\tr\var(d)=0\). So \(\var(Y)=\E Y^2-(\E Y)^2=-(\E Y)^2\leqslant0\); as a variance is
\(\geqslant0\) (P1), \(\var(Y)=0\) and \(\E Y=0\). By (P1), \(Y=\E Y=0\) with probability \(1\), that is,
\(d=0\) and \(x=\mu+Cw\) with probability \(1\).

(c) Here \(\mu=0\) and \(\Sigma=BB'\) with \(B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\0&1\\1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) of rank
\(2\), so \(C=B\) will do. Then \(C'C=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\),
\((C'C)^{-1}=\frac13\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&-1\\-1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), and
\(C'x=(x_1+x_3,\ x_2+x_3)'=(2u_1+u_2,\ u_1+2u_2)'\), so
\(w=\frac13\bigl(2(2u_1+u_2)-(u_1+2u_2),\ -(2u_1+u_2)+2(u_1+2u_2)\bigr)'=(u_1,u_2)'=u\), and \(x=Cu\).
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{r11-pr-precision}
Let \(x\sim N_n(\mu,\Sigma)\) with \(n\geqslant3\) and \(\Sigma\) positive definite, and let \(\Omega=\Sigma^{-1}=(\omega_{ij})\),
the \textbf{precision matrix}. Partition \(x=(x_{(1)}',x_{(2)}')'\) with \(x_{(1)}=(x_1,x_2)'\) and
\(x_{(2)}=(x_3,\dots,x_n)'\), and partition \(\Sigma\) and \(\Omega\) conformally.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Show that \(\Omega_{11}=\Sigma_{11\cdot2}^{-1}\), with \(\Sigma_{11\cdot2}\) as in
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-conditional}.
\item Deduce that the partial correlation of \(x_1\) and \(x_2\) given \(x_3,\dots,x_n\) is
\(-\omega_{12}/\sqrt{\omega_{11}\omega_{22}}\). So \(x_1\) and \(x_2\) are conditionally uncorrelated given all the other
variables if and only if \(\omega_{12}=0\).
\item Show that \(\var(x_1\mid x_2,\dots,x_n)=1/\omega_{11}\).
\item Check that \(3\Sigma^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1&-3&0\\1&2&-3&0\\-3&-3&9&-3\\0&0&-3&6\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) for
the matrix \(\Sigma\) of Exercise~\ref{r11-ex-partial-correlation}, and compare (b) with that exercise. Which pairs
are conditionally uncorrelated given the other two variables?
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
(a) \(\Sigma_{22}\) is positive definite (Corollary~\ref{r8-cor-pd}(a)), and
\(\det\Sigma=\det\Sigma_{22}\det\Sigma_{11\cdot2}\) (Theorem~\ref{r5d-thm-schur-det}(b)) with \(\det\Sigma>0\), so
\(\Sigma_{11\cdot2}\) is invertible. By Problem~\ref{r5b-pr-partitioned-inverse}(b) with \(D=\Sigma_{22}\) and
\(F=\Sigma_{11\cdot2}\), the leading \(2\times2\) block of \(\Sigma^{-1}\) is \(F^{-1}\).

(b) \(\Omega\) is positive definite (Corollary~\ref{r8-cor-pd}(a)), so its principal submatrix \(\Omega_{11}\) is
positive definite, \(\omega_{11},\omega_{22}>0\) and \(\det\Omega_{11}=\omega_{11}\omega_{22}-\omega_{12}^2>0\). By (a)
and Proposition~\ref{r2-prop-inverse-2x2},
\[
\Sigma_{11\cdot2}=\Omega_{11}^{-1}=\frac1{\omega_{11}\omega_{22}-\omega_{12}^2}
\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{22}&-\omega_{12}\\-\omega_{12}&\omega_{11}\end{pmatrix},
\]
and the correlation computed from it is
\(-\omega_{12}/\sqrt{\omega_{22}\omega_{11}}\). It is \(0\) exactly when \(\omega_{12}=0\).

(c) Repeat (a) with the partition \(x_{(1)}=x_1\), \(x_{(2)}=(x_2,\dots,x_n)'\): the \((1,1)\) entry \(\omega_{11}\) of
\(\Omega\) is \(\Sigma_{11\cdot2}^{-1}\), so the conditional variance \(\Sigma_{11\cdot2}\)
(Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-conditional}(b)) is \(1/\omega_{11}\).

(d) Multiplying, \(\Sigma\cdot\frac13\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1&-3&0\\1&2&-3&0\\-3&-3&9&-3\\0&0&-3&6\end{smallmatrix}\right)=I_4\);
for instance row \(1\) of \(\Sigma\) times column \(1\) is \(\frac13(8+1-6+0)=1\), and times column \(3\) is
\(\frac13(-12-3+18-3)=0\). So \(\Omega=\Sigma^{-1}\) (Theorem~\ref{r2-thm-invertible}), and (b) gives the partial
correlation \(-1/\sqrt{2\cdot2}=-\frac12\) of \(x_1\) and \(x_2\) given \(x_3,x_4\), as in
Exercise~\ref{r11-ex-partial-correlation}(a). The zero entries \(\omega_{14}=\omega_{24}=0\) show, by (b) applied after
renumbering the variables, that \(x_1,x_4\) and \(x_2,x_4\) are conditionally uncorrelated given the other two
variables; the other four pairs are not.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{r11-pr-mean-cov-independent}
Let \(x_1,\dots,x_n\) (\(n\geqslant2\)) be independent with distribution \(N_p(\mu,\Sigma)\), and let \(\bar x\) and \(S\)
be as in Proposition~\ref{r11-prop-sample-moments}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Show that \(\cov(\bar x,\ x_i-\bar x)=0\) for every \(i\).
\item Show that \(\bar x\sim N_p(\mu,\frac1n\Sigma)\) and that \(\bar x\) and \(S\) are independent.
\item Let \(\Sigma\) be positive definite. Show that \(n(\bar x-\mu)'\Sigma^{-1}(\bar x-\mu)\sim\chi^2_p\), independently
of \(S\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
(a) Let \(u=(x_1',\dots,x_n')'\). By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(d), \(u\) is normal with covariance
matrix \(V=\diag(\Sigma,\dots,\Sigma)\). Let \(G=[I_p\ \cdots\ I_p]\in\R^{p\times np}\) and let \(E_i\in\R^{p\times np}\)
have the block \(I_p\) in position \(i\) and zero blocks elsewhere; so \(\bar x=\frac1nGu\) and \(x_i=E_iu\). By the
block product (Theorem~\ref{r5b-thm-block}(c)), \(GVE_i'=\Sigma\) and \(GVG'=n\Sigma\). By
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-moments}(b),
\[
\cov\bigl(\bar x,\ x_i-\bar x\bigr)=\frac1nGV\Bigl(E_i-\frac1nG\Bigr)'=\frac1n\Sigma-\frac1{n^2}n\Sigma=0
\]

(b) \(\bar x\sim N_p(\mu,\frac1{n^2}GVG')=N_p(\mu,\frac1n\Sigma)\) by Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a). Let \(K\) have the
block rows \(E_1-\frac1nG,\dots,E_n-\frac1nG\), so that \(Ku=(x_1'-\bar x',\dots,x_n'-\bar x')'\). By (a), the cross
covariance \(\frac1nGVK'\) has zero blocks, so \(\bar x\indep Ku\) by Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(b). As
\(S=\frac1{n-1}\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)(x_i-\bar x)'\) is a function of \(Ku\), \(\bar x\indep S\) (P1).

(c) \(\frac1n\Sigma\) is positive definite with inverse \(n\Sigma^{-1}\), so
Corollary~\ref{r11-cor-chisq}(b) applied to \(\bar x\sim N_p(\mu,\frac1n\Sigma)\) gives the distribution \(\chi^2_p\). The
statistic is a function of \(\bar x\), hence independent of \(S\) by (b) and (P1).
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{r11-pr-equicorrelated}
Let \(n\geqslant2\), \(\sigma>0\), \(-1/(n-1)\leqslant\rho<1\), \(R=(1-\rho)I_n+\rho J_n\), and
\(x\sim N_n(\mu\one,\sigma^2R)\): equicorrelated observations.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Show that \(\sigma^2R\) is positive semidefinite.
\item Find the distributions of \(\bar x\) and of \(C_nx\), and show that \(\bar x\indep C_nx\); hence
\(\bar x\indep s^2\), where \(s^2=\frac1{n-1}\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)^2\).
\item Show that \((n-1)s^2/\bigl(\sigma^2(1-\rho)\bigr)\sim\chi^2_{n-1}\).
\item Let \(\rho>-1/(n-1)\). Show that \(T=\sqrt n(\bar x-\mu)/s\) has the distribution of \(\kappa V\), where
\(V\sim t_{n-1}\) and \(\kappa=\sqrt{(1+(n-1)\rho)/(1-\rho)}\). For \(n=9\) and \(\rho=\frac14\), show that the interval of
Example~\ref{r11-exm-t-interval} with level \(1-\alpha\) contains \(\mu\) with probability less than \(1-\alpha\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
(a) By Example~\ref{r7-exm-special}, the eigenvalues of \(R\), listed with multiplicity, are
\(1+(n-1)\rho\) and \(1-\rho\) (\(n-1\) times); both are \(\geqslant0\) here. So \(R\), and with it \(\sigma^2R\), is
positive semidefinite (Theorem~\ref{r8-thm-definite-eigen}(b)).

(b) Each row of \(R\) has sum \(1+(n-1)\rho\), so \(R\one=(1+(n-1)\rho)\one\) and \(\one'R=(1+(n-1)\rho)\one'\).
By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a),(b),
\(\bar x=\frac1n\one'x\sim N\bigl(\mu,\frac{\sigma^2}{n^2}\one'R\one\bigr)=N\bigl(\mu,\frac{\sigma^2}n(1+(n-1)\rho)\bigr)\), and
\(C_nx\sim N_n(0,\sigma^2C_nRC_n)\), where \(C_n\mu\one=0\) and
\(C_nRC_n=(1-\rho)C_n^2+\rho\,C_n\one\one'C_n=(1-\rho)C_n\). By
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(b) with \(A=\frac1n\one'\) and \(B=C_n\),
\(A(\sigma^2R)B'=\frac{\sigma^2}n(1+(n-1)\rho)\one'C_n=0\), so \(\bar x\indep C_nx\). As
\(s^2=\lVert C_nx\rVert^2/(n-1)\) is a function of \(C_nx\), \(\bar x\indep s^2\) (P1).

(c) Let \(z\sim N_n(0,I_n)\). Then \(\sigma\sqrt{1-\rho}\,C_nz\sim N_n\bigl(0,\sigma^2(1-\rho)C_n\bigr)\), the
distribution of \(C_nx\). The distribution of a function of a random vector depends only on the
distribution of the vector (Remark~\ref{r11-rem-probability}), so
\((n-1)s^2/(\sigma^2(1-\rho))=\lVert C_nx\rVert^2/(\sigma^2(1-\rho))\) has the distribution of
\(\lVert C_nz\rVert^2=z'C_nz\), which is \(\chi^2_{n-1}\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-chisq}).

(d) Let \(u=\sqrt n(\bar x-\mu)/\bigl(\sigma\sqrt{1+(n-1)\rho}\bigr)\sim N(0,1)\) and
\(v=(n-1)s^2/(\sigma^2(1-\rho))\sim\chi^2_{n-1}\); they are independent by (b) and (P1). Then
\[
\frac u{\sqrt{v/(n-1)}}=\frac{\sqrt n(\bar x-\mu)}{\sigma\sqrt{1+(n-1)\rho}}\cdot\frac{\sigma\sqrt{1-\rho}}s=\frac T\kappa,
\]
so \(T=\kappa V\) with \(V=u/\sqrt{v/(n-1)}\sim t_{n-1}\) (P5). For \(n=9\) and \(\rho=\frac14\),
\(\kappa^2=(1+2)/\frac34=4\) and \(\kappa=2\). With \(c=t_{8,1-\alpha/2}>0\) (Example~\ref{r11-exm-t-interval}), the interval
contains \(\mu\) exactly when \(\lvert T\rvert\leqslant c\), that is, \(\lvert V\rvert\leqslant\frac c2\). As in
Example~\ref{r11-exm-t-interval}, \(-V\sim t_8\), so \(\Prob(V<-\frac c2)=\Prob(V>\frac c2)\) and
\(\Prob(\lvert V\rvert\leqslant\frac c2)=2\Prob(V\leqslant\frac c2)-1\). The distribution function of \(t_8\) is strictly
increasing (P5) and \(\frac c2<c\), so \(\Prob(V\leqslant\frac c2)<1-\frac\alpha2\) and the probability is less than
\(2(1-\frac\alpha2)-1=1-\alpha\): with positively correlated observations the interval of
Example~\ref{r11-exm-t-interval} is too short.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{r11-pr-two-sample}
Let \(x_1,\dots,x_m\) be \(N(\mu_1,\sigma^2)\) and \(y_1,\dots,y_n\) be \(N(\mu_2,\sigma^2)\), all \(m+n\) independent,
with \(m,n\geqslant2\) and \(\sigma>0\), and let
\(s_p^2=\bigl(\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)^2+\sum_j(y_j-\bar y)^2\bigr)/(m+n-2)\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item Show that \(T=\dfrac{\bar x-\bar y-(\mu_1-\mu_2)}{s_p\sqrt{1/m+1/n}}\sim t_{m+n-2}\).
\item Deduce a confidence interval with level \(1-\alpha\) for \(\mu_1-\mu_2\).
\item Four plants given a new fertilizer have heights \(12,15,13,16\), and five control plants \(10,9,12,11,8\).
Compute \(T\) for \(\mu_1=\mu_2\) and the interval of (b).
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
(a)
\begin{itemize}
\item By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-independence}(d), \(v=(x_1,\dots,x_m,y_1,\dots,y_n)'\) is normal with mean
\((\mu_1\one_m',\mu_2\one_n')'\) and covariance matrix \(\sigma^2I_{m+n}\); so \(\varepsilon=v-\E v\sim N_{m+n}(0,\sigma^2I_{m+n})\).
\item \(\bar x-\bar y-(\mu_1-\mu_2)=a'\varepsilon\) with \(a=(\frac1m\one_m',-\frac1n\one_n')'\), \(a'a=\frac1m+\frac1n\).
\item As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-one-sample}, \((m+n-2)s_p^2=\varepsilon'P\varepsilon\) with
\(P=\diag(C_m,C_n)\). \(P\) is symmetric and idempotent (Proposition~\ref{r5b-prop-block-tri}(b)), of rank
\((m-1)+(n-1)\) (Theorem~\ref{thm:r4-partitioned}(c), Corollary~\ref{r6-cor-centering}(b)).
\item \(a'P=(\frac1m\one_m'C_m,\ -\frac1n\one_n'C_n)=0\), so \(a'\varepsilon\indep\varepsilon'P\varepsilon\)
(Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-quadratic-independence}(c)). Moreover \(u=a'\varepsilon/(\sigma\sqrt{a'a})\sim N(0,1)\) and
\(w=\varepsilon'P\varepsilon/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2_{m+n-2}\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(b),
Corollary~\ref{r11-cor-chisq}(a)), and \(T=u/\sqrt{w/(m+n-2)}\sim t_{m+n-2}\) by (P1) and (P5).
\end{itemize}

(b) Exactly as in Example~\ref{r11-exm-t-interval}, with \(c=t_{m+n-2,1-\alpha/2}\),
\(\Prob(-c\leqslant T\leqslant c)=1-\alpha\), so \(\bar x-\bar y\pm c\,s_p\sqrt{1/m+1/n}\) is a confidence interval
with level \(1-\alpha\) for \(\mu_1-\mu_2\).

(c) \(\bar x=14\), \(\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)^2=4+1+1+4=10\); \(\bar y=10\), \(\sum_j(y_j-\bar y)^2=0+1+4+1+4=10\). So
\(s_p^2=\frac{20}7\), \(s_p^2(\frac14+\frac15)=\frac{20}7\cdot\frac9{20}=\frac97\), and
\(s_p\sqrt{1/m+1/n}=3/\sqrt7\). For \(\mu_1=\mu_2\), \(T=4\sqrt7/3\). The interval is
\(\bigl[4-\frac3{\sqrt7}t_{7,1-\alpha/2},\ 4+\frac3{\sqrt7}t_{7,1-\alpha/2}\bigr]\); it contains \(0\) exactly when
\(t_{7,1-\alpha/2}\geqslant4\sqrt7/3\).
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{r11-pr-chisq-converse}
Let \(z\sim N_n(0,I_n)\), and let \(A\in\R^{n\times n}\) be symmetric with \(z'Az\sim\chi^2_r\) for some
\(r\geqslant1\). Show that \(A\) is idempotent of rank \(r\): the converse of Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-chisq}.
(Compare MGFs, and use Remark~\ref{r7-rem-polynomials}.)
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
\begin{itemize}
\item By Theorem~\ref{r8-thm-spectral}(a), \(A=Q\Lambda Q'\) with \(Q\) orthogonal; by
Corollary~\ref{r11-cor-orthogonal-invariance}, \(w=Q'z\) has independent \(N(0,1)\) entries, and
\(z'Az=w'\Lambda w=\sum_i\lambda_iw_i^2\).
\item Each \(w_i^2\sim\chi^2_1\), with MGF \((1-2s)^{-1/2}\) for \(s<\frac12\) (P5). Choose \(\delta>0\) with
\(\delta<\frac12\) and \(2\lvert\lambda_i\rvert\delta<1\) for all \(i\). For \(\lvert t\rvert<\delta\), the factors
\(e^{t\lambda_iw_i^2}\) are functions of the independent \(w_i\) with finite means, so by (P1)
\[
\E e^{t\,z'Az}=\prod_{i=1}^n\E e^{(t\lambda_i)w_i^2}=\prod_{i=1}^n(1-2\lambda_it)^{-1/2}
\]
\item As \(z'Az\sim\chi^2_r\), the left side is also \((1-2t)^{-r/2}\). Both sides are positive; raising them to the
power \(-2\) gives \(\prod_i(1-2\lambda_it)=(1-2t)^r\) for \(\lvert t\rvert<\delta\). Two polynomials that agree at
infinitely many points are equal (Remark~\ref{r7-rem-polynomials}, (A1)), so this holds for all \(t\).
\item If \(\lambda_i\ne0\), then \(t=1/(2\lambda_i)\) is a root of the left side, hence of the right side, which
vanishes only at \(t=\frac12\); so \(\lambda_i=1\). Thus every \(\lambda_i\in\{0,1\}\), and if \(m\) of them equal
\(1\), then \((1-2t)^m=(1-2t)^r\); comparing degrees, \(m=r\).
\item So \(\Lambda^2=\Lambda\), \(A^2=Q\Lambda Q'Q\Lambda Q'=Q\Lambda^2Q'=A\), and \(\rank A=r\), the number of nonzero
\(\lambda_i\) (Corollary~\ref{r8-cor-spectral-decomposition}(b)).\qedhere
\end{itemize}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{r11-pr-multinomial}
In \(N\geqslant2\) independent trials, each trial results in exactly one of \(k\geqslant2\) categories, category \(j\)
with probability \(p_j>0\) (\(p_1+\dots+p_k=1\)). Let \(y_i\in\R^k\) be the indicator vector of trial \(i\)
(\(y_i=e_j\) if trial \(i\) results in category \(j\)), \(c=y_1+\dots+y_N\) the vector of counts,
\(p=(p_1,\dots,p_k)'\) and \(D=\diag(p_1,\dots,p_k)\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item Show that \(\E c=Np\) and \(\var(c)=NV\) with \(V=D-pp'\).
\item Show that \(V\one=0\), \(\mathcal N(V)=\Span\{\one\}\) and \(\rank V=k-1\). Explain \(V\one=0\) by
Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-cov-psd}.
\item Show that \(D^{-1}\) is a generalized inverse of \(V\) (Definition~\ref{r10-def-ginverse}).
\item Let \(u=(c-Np)/\sqrt N\). Show that \textbf{Pearson's statistic}
\(X^2=\sum_j(c_j-Np_j)^2/(Np_j)\) equals \(u'D^{-1}u\), and that \(u'Gu=X^2\) for every generalized inverse \(G\)
of \(V\).
\item Suppose, as an approximation for large \(N\), that \(u\sim N_k(0,V)\). Show that then
\(X^2\sim\chi^2_{k-1}\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
(a) Entry \(j\) of \(y_i\) is \(1\) with probability \(p_j\) and \(0\) otherwise, so \(\E y_i=p\) (P1). As \(y_i\) is a unit
vector, \(y_iy_i'=\diag(y_i)\), so \(\E(y_iy_i')=D\) and \(\var(y_i)=D-pp'=V\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-moments}(c)).
The \(y_i\) are independent, hence uncorrelated (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-moments}(e)). By
Proposition~\ref{r11-prop-sample-moments} applied to \(\bar y=c/N\), \(\E c=N\E\bar y=Np\) and
\(\var(c)=N^2\var(\bar y)=N^2V/N=NV\).

(b) \(V\one=p-p(p'\one)=p-p=0\). If \(Vx=0\), then \(Dx=p(p'x)\), so \(x_j=p'x\) for every \(j\) (divide by
\(p_j>0\)), and \(x=(p'x)\one\). Hence \(\mathcal N(V)=\Span\{\one\}\) and \(\rank V=k-1\)
(Theorem~\ref{thm:r4-rank-nullity}). Every trial falls in exactly one category, so \(\one'c=N=\one'\E c\)
always and \(\var(\one'c)=0\) (P1). By Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-cov-psd}(a), \(\one'\var(c)\one=0\) and \(\var(c)\)
is positive semidefinite, hence \(\var(c)\one=0\) (Proposition~\ref{r8-prop-psd}(a)); as \(\var(c)=NV\), this is
\(V\one=0\). So \(\var(c)\) is singular, as Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-cov-psd}(b) with \(a=\one\) says.

(c) As \(p'D^{-1}=\one'\), \(VD^{-1}=I_k-p\one'\); as \(\one'D=p'\) and \(\one'p=1\),
\[
VD^{-1}V=(I_k-p\one')(D-pp')=D-pp'-pp'+p(\one'p)p'=D-pp'=V
\]

(d) \(u'D^{-1}u=\sum_ju_j^2/p_j=\sum_j(c_j-Np_j)^2/(Np_j)=X^2\). Next \(\one'u=(\one'c-N)/\sqrt N=0\), so
\(u\in W=\Span\{\one\}^\perp\), a subspace of dimension \(k-1\) (Theorem~\ref{r6-thm-complement}(c)). It contains
\(\mathcal C(V)\), since \(\one'Vw=(V\one)'w=0\) for every \(w\in\R^k\), and \(\dim\mathcal C(V)=\rank V=k-1\) by (b);
so \(\mathcal C(V)=W\) (Theorem~\ref{thm:r4-counting}(a)), and \(u=Vw\) for some \(w\in\R^k\). For every generalized
inverse \(G\) of \(V\), \(u'Gu=w'VGVw=w'Vw\), the same number for all \(G\); by (c) it equals \(u'D^{-1}u=X^2\).

(e) Let \(q=(\sqrt{p_1},\dots,\sqrt{p_k})'\), so \(q'q=1\), and \(D^{-1/2}=\diag(p_1^{-1/2},\dots,p_k^{-1/2})\). Then
\(D^{-1/2}VD^{-1/2}=I_k-qq'=P\), symmetric and idempotent (\(P^2=I_k-2qq'+q(q'q)q'=P\)) of rank \(\tr P=k-1\)
(Theorem~\ref{r6-thm-idempotent}(c)). So \(v=D^{-1/2}u\sim N_k(0,P)\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-mvn-linear}(a)). For
\(z\sim N_k(0,I_k)\), \(Pz\sim N_k(0,PP')=N_k(0,P)\) as well; hence \(X^2=v'v\) has the distribution of
\((Pz)'(Pz)=z'Pz\), which is \(\chi^2_{k-1}\) (Theorem~\ref{r11-thm-chisq}).
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{r11-pr-mendel}
In an experiment of Mendel's type, \(N=160\) pea seeds are classified as round yellow, round green, wrinkled
yellow and wrinkled green, with counts \(c=(93,\ 27,\ 31,\ 9)'\). Mendel's theory predicts the probabilities
\(p=\frac1{16}(9,3,3,1)'\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item Compute the expected counts \(Np_j\), the contributions \((c_j-Np_j)^2/(Np_j)\) and Pearson's \(X^2\)
(Problem~\ref{r11-pr-multinomial}).
\item Under the approximation of Problem~\ref{r11-pr-multinomial}(e), \(X^2\sim\chi^2_3\). Show that then
\(\Prob(X^2>\frac8{15})>\frac12\), and comment on the fit of the theory. (Compare \(z_1^2+z_2^2+z_3^2\) with
\(z_1^2+z_2^2\).)
\item With \(V\) as in Problem~\ref{r11-pr-multinomial}, let \(V_{11}\) be its leading \(3\times3\) block and
\(D_3=\diag(p_1,p_2,p_3)\). Show that \(V_{11}^{-1}=D_3^{-1}+\frac1{p_4}\one_3\one_3'\), so that
\(G=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}V_{11}^{-1}&0\\0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) is a generalized inverse of \(V\)
(Theorem~\ref{r10-thm-ginverse-exist}(b)); and check directly that \(u'Gu=X^2\) for these data.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
(a) The expected counts are \(160\cdot\frac9{16}=90\), \(160\cdot\frac3{16}=30\) (twice) and \(160\cdot\frac1{16}=10\), and
\(c-Np=(3,-3,1,-1)'\). The contributions are
\[
\frac9{90}=\frac1{10},\qquad\frac9{30}=\frac3{10},\qquad\frac1{30},\qquad\frac1{10},
\]
and \(X^2=\frac{3+9+1+3}{30}=\frac8{15}\).

(b) Let \(z_1,z_2,z_3\) be independent \(N(0,1)\). By (P5), \(v_3=z_1^2+z_2^2+z_3^2\sim\chi^2_3\) and
\(v_2=z_1^2+z_2^2\sim\chi^2_2\), and \(v_3\geqslant v_2\). So the event \(\{v_2>c\}\) is contained in \(\{v_3>c\}\), and by
(P5), \(\Prob(v_3>c)\geqslant\Prob(v_2>c)=e^{-c/2}\). For \(c=\frac8{15}\), \(e^{-4/15}>\frac12\), since
\(e^4<3^4=81<2^{15}\). So under the theory a value of \(X^2\) larger than the observed one has probability more than
\(\frac12\): the counts agree well with Mendel's \(9:3:3:1\) law.

(c) The leading block is \(V_{11}=D_3-p_{(3)}p_{(3)}'\) with \(p_{(3)}=(p_1,p_2,p_3)'\). As
\(p_{(3)}'D_3^{-1}=\one_3'\), \(D_3\one_3=p_{(3)}\) and \(\one_3'p_{(3)}=p_1+p_2+p_3=1-p_4\),
\[
\bigl(D_3-p_{(3)}p_{(3)}'\bigr)\Bigl(D_3^{-1}+\frac1{p_4}\one_3\one_3'\Bigr)
=I_3-p_{(3)}\one_3'+\frac1{p_4}\bigl(p_{(3)}-p_{(3)}(1-p_4)\bigr)\one_3'=I_3,
\]
so \(V_{11}\) is invertible with the stated inverse (Theorem~\ref{r2-thm-invertible}). As \(\rank V=3\)
(Problem~\ref{r11-pr-multinomial}(b)), \(G\) is a generalized inverse of \(V\)
(Theorem~\ref{r10-thm-ginverse-exist}(b)). With \(u\) as in Problem~\ref{r11-pr-multinomial}(d) and \(u_{(3)}=(u_1,u_2,u_3)'\),
\[
u'Gu=u_{(3)}'V_{11}^{-1}u_{(3)}=\sum_{j\leqslant3}\frac{u_j^2}{p_j}+\frac1{p_4}\Bigl(\sum_{j\leqslant3}u_j\Bigr)^2
=\sum_{j=1}^4\frac{u_j^2}{p_j}=X^2,
\]
since \(\sum_{j\leqslant3}u_j=-u_4\). For these data, \(\sum_{j\leqslant3}(c_j-Np_j)=3-3+1=1=-(c_4-Np_4)\), and
\(u'Gu=\frac1{10}+\frac3{10}+\frac1{30}+\frac1{160\cdot\frac1{16}}=\frac8{15}\), as Problem~\ref{r11-pr-multinomial}(d) predicts.
\end{solution}
\clearpage
\def\unitnumber{12}
\setcounter{theorem}{0}\setcounter{exercise}{0}\setcounter{problem}{0}
\section*{Chapter 12. Linear models and generalized linear models}

\subsection*{Least squares}

In the \textbf{linear regression model}, the response vector \(y=(y_1,\dots,y_n)'\) holds \(n\)
observations, and \(x_{ij}\) is the value of the \(j\)th explanatory variable on observation \(i\)
(\(1\leqslant j\leqslant k\)):
\[
y_i=\beta_0+\beta_1x_{i1}+\dots+\beta_kx_{ik}+\varepsilon_i\quad(1\leqslant i\leqslant n),\qquad\text{that is,}\qquad
y=X\beta+\varepsilon
\]
The \textbf{design matrix} \(X=[\one_n\ X_0]\in\R^{n\times p}\), \(p=k+1\), has the data matrix \(X_0=(x_{ij})\)
(Example~\ref{r1-exm-data}) as its last \(k\) columns and \(i\)th row \(x_i'=(1,x_{i1},\dots,x_{ik})\);
\(\beta=(\beta_0,\dots,\beta_k)'\), the coefficient \(\beta_0\) of \(\one_n\) is the \textbf{intercept}, and
\(\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_1,\dots,\varepsilon_n)'\) is the vector of errors. For any \(X\in\R^{n\times p}\) with rows
\(x_i'\) and any \(y\in\R^n\), a \textbf{least-squares estimate} of \(\beta\) is a vector \(\hat\beta\in\R^p\) that
minimizes
\[
S(\beta)=\lVert y-X\beta\rVert^2=\sum_{i=1}^n(y_i-x_i'\beta)^2\qquad(\beta\in\R^p);
\]
then \(\hat y=X\hat\beta\) is the vector of \textbf{fitted values}, \(e=y-\hat y\) the vector of \textbf{residuals}, and
\(\mathrm{SSE}=S(\hat\beta)=e'e\), the minimum of \(S\), the \textbf{error sum of squares}. The \(p\) equations
\(X'X\beta=X'y\) are the \textbf{normal equations}. The results below hold for every \(X\in\R^{n\times p}\), with or
without the column \(\one_n\), unless an intercept is assumed.

\begin{theorem}\label{r12-thm-normal-equations}
Let \(X\in\R^{n\times p}\) and \(y\in\R^n\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item The normal equations \(X'X\beta=X'y\) (unknown \(\beta\in\R^p\)) are consistent.
\item \(X'X\) is invertible if and only if \(X\) has full column rank, \(\rank X=p\). Then the normal
equations have exactly one solution, \(\hat\beta=(X'X)^{-1}X'y\).
\item If \(\rank X<p\) and \(\hat\beta\) is one solution, the solutions are the vectors \(\hat\beta+z\) with
\(z\in\mathcal N(X)\); there are infinitely many, and all of them give the same vector \(X\hat\beta\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(a) \(X'y\in\mathcal C(X')=\mathcal C(X'X)\) (Theorem~\ref{thm:r4-rank-ata}(c)), so the system is consistent
(Corollary~\ref{cor:r4-ax-b}(a)).

(b) \(X'X\in\R^{p\times p}\) is invertible if and only if \(\rank(X'X)=p\)
(Theorem~\ref{thm:r4-rank-rules}(d)), that is, \(\rank X=p\) (Theorem~\ref{thm:r4-rank-ata}(b)). Then
multiplying \(X'X\beta=X'y\) by \((X'X)^{-1}\) gives \(\beta=(X'X)^{-1}X'y\), and this vector is a solution.

(c) By Theorem~\ref{thm:r2-solution-structure}(a), the solutions are \(\hat\beta+z\) with
\(z\in\mathcal N(X'X)=\mathcal N(X)\) (Theorem~\ref{thm:r4-rank-ata}(a)), and
\(X(\hat\beta+z)=X\hat\beta+Xz=X\hat\beta\). As \(\rank X<p\), some \(z\ne0\) lies in \(\mathcal N(X)\)
(Theorem~\ref{thm:r4-rank-rules}(b)), and the solutions \(\hat\beta+tz\) (\(t\in\R\)) are pairwise distinct,
since \(t_1z=t_2z\) forces \(t_1=t_2\).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{r12-thm-hat}
